| v5.9.84 | **Lazy + progressive image loading — an image-heavy page shows its text instantly and only downloads the pictures you can actually see.** v5.9.83 fetched *all* of a page's `<img>`s synchronously before showing anything, so an image-heavy page stalled on every picture. Now `selene_load_ex` **renders and paints the page first** (`compositor_redraw_now`, images as placeholders), then **`selene_fetch_visible`** downloads only the images whose box is currently on-screen — redrawing after each so they **pop in one at a time**. Scrolling calls **`selene_after_scroll`** (repaint immediately, then fetch any newly-visible images), so off-screen pictures are fetched **only when you scroll to them**. A per-image **`tried`** flag means a fetched or failed (e.g. 404) image is never re-requested. Refactored into `selene_fetch_one` (download+decode one image, with the v5.9.83 retry), `selene_img_anchor` (which line an image's box sits on), `selene_fetch_visible`, and `selene_after_scroll`. **Verified live in the GUI, 0 faults** (two screenshots + the server log): a **taller-than-a-screen** page with three images — on load, the **text appears immediately** and **only the one visible image is downloaded** (the server logs exactly one `GET /nyx.png` per page load, images two and three untouched); after **scrolling down**, images two and three are fetched **as they enter the viewport** (two further `GET /nyx.png`) and render in place. **Honest scope:** each individual fetch is still synchronous (it briefly blocks the UI while that one image downloads — true background fetching would need a worker thread), but the page no longer waits on off-screen images, which was the actual stall. **No new syscalls** (still 57); no new files; `nyx-kernel.bin` 1611.5 -> 1612.3 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** more image formats (BMP/GIF, or the bigger JPEG), a background-thread fetch for true non-blocking loads, or back to TLS (`0x0601`) / other Selene polish. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~16 out.) |
| v5.9.83 | **Selene draws real images — the payoff of the image arc: `<img>` PNGs are now fetched, decoded and rendered inline.** v5.9.81 (inflate) + v5.9.82 (PNG decode) built the pipeline; this wires it into the browser. After a page renders, **`selene_fetch_images`** downloads the first few `<img>` sources (HTTP or TLS, same machinery as the page fetch), and `png_decode`s each into RGBA stored on the `sel_img_t`. Images become **block-level boxes** (`SEL_IMG_BOX_W`×`SEL_IMG_BOX_LINES` = 192×90 px) that reserve their own vertical space; a decoded image is **aspect-fit scaled** (nearest-neighbour) and **alpha-composited** over the page, while anything that fails to fetch/decode (or isn't PNG) keeps the framed **`[img: alt]`** placeholder. Decoded pixels are freed on navigation and tab-close; the fetch is bounded (≤ 8 images/page, ≤ 512 KB each). **A real bug the live test caught + fixed:** the *first* new TCP connection right after the page fetch sometimes fails, so with two identical `<img src>` only the second rendered — the fetch now **retries once**, and both come through. **Verified live in the GUI, 0 faults** (screenshot): a page served to the guest with a 220×150 PNG (a purple gradient + white crescent moon + stars) renders **both copies as the actual image, scaled into their boxes**, with ordinary text flowing above and below, and a deliberately-missing `/missing.png` correctly showing **`[img: missing image]`** — the server log confirms `GET /` then two `GET /nyx.png` (200) + `GET /missing.png` (404). **So NyxOS's browser now shows real pictures.** **Honest scope:** PNG only (per `png.c`: 8-bit, non-interlaced), the image fetch is synchronous (it blocks the page load briefly), and the display box is a fixed size. **No new syscalls** (still 57); no new files; `nyx-kernel.bin` 1609.8 -> 1611.5 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** more image polish (JPEG/GIF/BMP, or lazy/async fetch so image-heavy pages don't stall the load), or back to TLS (`0x0601`) / other Selene work. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~17 out.) |
| v5.9.82 | **A PNG decoder (`png.c`) on top of v5.9.81's inflate — NyxOS can now turn PNG bytes into pixels.** Step two of the real-image arc. New **`kernel/png.c` + `png.h`** decode the common PNG shapes the web uses: it verifies the 8-byte signature, walks the **chunk** stream (`IHDR` geometry, `PLTE` palette, `tRNS` transparency, `IDAT`, `IEND`), **concatenates the IDAT payloads** and `zlib_inflate`s them (v5.9.81), reverses the **per-scanline filters** — all five: None / Sub / Up / Average / **Paeth** — and expands every pixel to a flat **RGBA** buffer. Supported: **8-bit depth, non-interlaced**, color types **grayscale (0) / RGB (2) / palette (3, incl. `tRNS` per-index alpha) / gray+alpha (4) / RGBA (6)**. `png_decode()` returns a `png_image_t { width, height, pixels }` (kmalloc'd RGBA, freed by `png_free`); it bounds every allocation (≤ 4096 per side, ≤ 1M pixels) and fails safe on a malformed or unsupported stream. **Pinned by a new `pngtest`, 5/5:** five embedded PNGs — an **RGB** 4×4, an **RGBA** 3×3, a **palette+tRNS** 4×2, a **grayscale** 2×2, and a 5×5 **RGB image whose rows use all five filter types** — each decode to the **exact expected RGBA**, byte for byte. The vectors were produced by Python (scratchpad/gen_png.py) and independently **cross-checked against Pillow**. **0 faults.** (The inflate self-test still passes 5/5 — no regression.) **Honest scope:** no 16-bit or sub-byte (1/2/4-bit) depth, no Adam7 interlacing, and chunk **CRC-32 is not verified** (the zlib Adler-32 already covers the IDAT payload) — all return an error or are skipped rather than mis-decoded. **No new syscalls** (still 57); new command **`pngtest`**; new files **`kernel/png.c` + `kernel/png.h`** (git add); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1604.9 -> 1609.8 KB. **Next — the payoff:** wire this into Selene's `<img>` — fetch the image bytes over HTTP/TLS, `png_decode`, and draw the pixels scaled into the placeholder box (reusing the `img_of` overlay from v5.9.76). (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~18 out.) |
| v5.9.81 | **DEFLATE / zlib decompression (`inflate.c`) — the first step toward decoding real images in Selene.** Selene draws `<img>` as `[img: alt]` placeholders (v5.9.76) because NyxOS has no image decoder. The single biggest prerequisite for real images is **DEFLATE decompression**: every PNG's pixel data is a zlib-wrapped DEFLATE stream (IDAT). This lands a compact, allocation-free inflater — new **`kernel/inflate.c` + `inflate.h`** in the style of Mark Adler's public-domain `puff.c`: an LSB-first **bit reader**, canonical-**Huffman** table build/decode, and all three RFC 1951 block types — **stored** (uncompressed), **fixed** and **dynamic** Huffman — including the LZ77 length/distance **back-reference** copies. **`inflate_raw`** does raw DEFLATE; **`zlib_inflate`** adds the RFC 1950 framing PNG uses — the 2-byte header (CM/checkbits) and the trailing **Adler-32**, which it recomputes and verifies. It's written to fail safe on malformed input (every write bounded by the output cap, a sticky underflow flag) since these streams will come off the network. **Pinned by a new `deflatetest`, 5/5:** a **dynamic**-Huffman blob, a **fixed**-Huffman blob (`Z_FIXED`), a **stored** blob (level 0), and a **zlib-wrapped + Adler-32** case all round-trip to the exact expected bytes, and a **one-bit-corrupted Adler-32 is rejected** — vectors produced by Python's `zlib` (scratchpad/gen_inflate.py), one per block type. **0 faults.** This is a pure primitive (no network, no window) — the payoff comes when it's wired up: **next is a PNG decoder** (IHDR/PLTE/IDAT parsing → `zlib_inflate` → un-filter scanlines → RGB pixels), then hooking it into Selene's `<img>` to draw real images scaled into the placeholder box. **No new syscalls** (still 57); new command **`deflatetest`**; new files **`kernel/inflate.c` + `kernel/inflate.h`** (git add); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1598.2 -> 1604.9 KB. **Next:** the PNG decoder on top of this, or (smaller) a fuller CA bundle / other polish. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~19 out.) |
| v5.9.80 | **secp384r1 as an ECDHE group + P-384 ECDHE key agreement — which turns v5.9.79's KAT-only ECDSA-P384 check into a LIVE, end-to-end verification.** v5.9.79 could verify an ECDSA-P384 ServerKeyExchange signature but had never done so against a real server: the two reachable P-384-leaf sites answered our ClientHello with `handshake_failure`. The cause turned out to be the **supported_groups** extension: per RFC 4492 a server with a P-384 ECDSA certificate wants **`secp384r1` (0x18)** advertised there before it will proceed — even if the key exchange itself uses another curve. So this adds `0x18` to supported_groups (after x25519 + P-256, which stay preferred) **and** wires up **P-384 ECDHE key agreement**: the ECDHE block is now fully curve-generic — for P-384 it draws a 48-byte CSPRNG scalar `d`, sends our point **`d·G`** (`p384_base_scalar` → the 97-byte `0x04‖X‖Y`), and derives the **pre-master from the X-coordinate of `d·serverPub`** (`p384_point_scalar`), feeding a now-variable-length pre-master (32 or 48 bytes) into the key schedule; the ClientKeyExchange / transcript / flight buffers grew to fit a 97-byte point. **The payoff — verified live, 0 faults:** **`tls ecc384.badssl.com`** (a P-384-ECDSA-cert server that refused us one version ago) now **completes the full handshake and prints `ServerKeyExchange signature VERIFIED against the leaf certificate (ECDSA-P384)`** + `HANDSHAKE COMPLETE` + a successful fetch — the exact live 0x0503 verification v5.9.79 left open. **Regressions intact:** `tls example.com` still `ECDHE(x25519)` + `ECDSA-P256`, and `tls httpbin.org` still `ECDHE(secp256r1 (P-256))` + `RSA-PKCS1` — the curve-generic rework didn't disturb the existing paths. **Honest scope:** ecc384 (and every reachable host probed) does the *key exchange* over x25519/P-256, **refusing secp384r1 for ECDHE** — P-384 as an ECDHE group is essentially unused on today's web — so the new **P-384 ECDHE key-agreement path itself isn't exercised end-to-end by a live server**; its math is the same `p384_base_scalar`/`p384_point_scalar` already pinned **4/4 by `p384test`**, and its TLS wiring is byte-for-byte the structure of the live-proven P-256 branch. It's there for any server that does prefer it. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1597.9 -> 1598.2 KB (`tls.c`; the P-384 math already existed). **Next:** a fuller CA bundle, RSA-PKCS1-SHA512 SKE (`0x0601`, the last unverified scheme), or back to the Selene browser. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~20 out.) |
| v5.9.79 | **ECDSA-P384/SHA-384 ServerKeyExchange verification (`0x0503`) — the last common SKE signature scheme NyxOS couldn't check.** Back to TLS after the browser run. The SKE-signature verifier handled ECDSA-P256 (`0x0403`), RSA-PKCS1 (`0x0401`) and RSA-PSS (`0x0804`); a server signing the key exchange with **`ecdsa_secp384r1_sha384`** reached "proceed unverified". This adds it, reusing `p384.c` (v5.9.64) + `sha512.c`'s SHA-384 (v5.9.63). The ClientHello now **advertises `0x0503`** in `signature_algorithms` (so a P-384-leaf server will actually use it), and the SKE dispatch is refactored around a unified **`tls_ecdsa_leaf_verify(leaf, hash, hlen, sig)`** helper: `hlen` picks the curve — 32 → P-256 (65-byte point) or 48 → P-384 (97-byte point) — extracting the leaf's EC key, parsing the DER `SEQUENCE{r,s}` (via a generalized `der_int_to_n`), and calling `p256_ecdsa_verify` / `p384_ecdsa_verify`. For `0x0503` the signed digest is **`SHA-384(client_random ‖ server_random ‖ ServerECDHParams)`**. The existing P-256 path now flows through the same helper (a small de-dup). **Pinned by a new `skp384test`, 3/3:** it extracts the **P-384 public key from a real leaf certificate**, then accepts a valid **ECDSA-P384/SHA-384 signature over a known 48-byte digest** and rejects a one-bit-tampered digest (cert + signature generated by a standard library). **Regression verified live, 0 faults:** `tls example.com` still prints **`ServerKeyExchange signature VERIFIED … (ECDSA-P256)`** and completes the fetch (the refactor didn't disturb the P-256/RSA paths). **Honest scope — no live 0x0503 run yet, and why:** the two reachable P-384-leaf servers (`ecc384.badssl.com`, `suche.org`) **require a P-384 ECDHE group** (`secp384r1`, `0x18`) for the key exchange, which NyxOS doesn't offer (only x25519 + P-256), so both answer our ClientHello with `handshake_failure`. The P-384 *signature* verifier is done and KAT-proven; exercising it end-to-end needs **P-384 ECDHE key agreement** — the natural next step (exactly as X25519/P-256 landed KAT'd before their ECDHE wiring in v5.9.51/70). So NyxOS now *verifies* four of the common TLS-1.2 SKE schemes — ECDSA-P256, **ECDSA-P384**, RSA-PKCS1, RSA-PSS (only RSA-PKCS1-SHA512 `0x0601` remains). **No new syscalls** (still 57); new `skp384test` command; `nyx-kernel.bin` 1596.0 -> 1597.9 KB (`tls.c`). **Next:** **P-384 ECDHE** (`0x18`) to unlock a live `ecc384.badssl.com` 0x0503 verify, a fuller CA bundle, or back to Selene. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~21 out.) |
| v5.9.78 | **Selene: tabs — several pages open at once in one window (the browser's biggest UX jump yet).** Until now a Selene window was a single page; opening a new site replaced the old one. Now each window holds up to **6 tabs**, each a **full independent page context** (its own URL, history, scroll, links, forms, find state). The window's `reserved` becomes a small **`selene_tabs_t` manager** (`tab[]` + `active`); the old per-page `selene_create_ctx` body is now `selene_new_ctx` (one tab), a new `selene_create_ctx` builds the manager with one tab, and `selene_free_ctx` tears a tab down. The three entry points (`selene_win_draw`/`_key`/`_click`) resolve `T->tab[T->active]` and are otherwise unchanged — everything below them still operates on a single `selene_ctx_t`, so tables/images/forms/find all keep working per-tab. A **tab strip** is drawn below the toolbar: one button per tab labelled by its page `<title>` (else host, else "New Tab"), the **active tab highlighted** (light fill + a purple top accent), a per-tab **close `x`**, and a **`+` new-tab button**; the page view shrinks by the strip height. **Controls:** click a tab to switch, its `x` to close, `+` to open; **Ctrl+T** new tab, **Ctrl+W** close, **Ctrl+Tab** / **Ctrl+PgDn** next, **Ctrl+PgUp** prev (via `is_ctrl_pressed()`). Closing never drops the last tab and keeps the active index sensible after the array shifts. **Pinned by `formtest`, now 7/7** (was 6/6): a new case exercises the manager — new opens+focuses, close shifts the active index correctly, and the last tab can't be closed. **Verified live in the GUI, 0 faults** (2 screenshots): a home page served to the guest opens in tab 1, **Ctrl+T** opens tab 2, and after tab 2 follows a link to `/planets.html` the strip reads **`Selene Home` | `Planets`** (different labels, tab 1 untouched) with each tab's own page + status — proof the contexts are independent. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1593.5 -> 1596.0 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** more Selene polish (per-tab loading/back within tabs is already there; could add drag-reorder, or more HTML entities) or back to TLS refinements (fuller CA bundle, ECDSA-P384 SKE `0x0503`). (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~22 out.) |
| v5.9.77 | **Selene: `<table>` column layout — tables stop collapsing into a run-on line and render as aligned, bordered columns.** Until now `<table>`/`<tr>` were just paragraph/line breaks and `<td>`/`<th>` fell through, so every table's cells ran together as one blob of text. This lays tables out properly. Because column widths can't be known until every row is seen, `<table>` is handled in **two passes**: `render_table` parses the whole table into a cell list (`sel_tag_match` recognizes `tr`/`td`/`th`/`table` open/close; `sel_cell_text` extracts each cell's text — inner tags stripped, entities decoded, whitespace collapsed), **measures each column's max width**, **clamps the total to fit** (`SEL_TBL_ROWCAP 84 < SEL_WRAP`, shrinking the widest column until the row fits so rows never word-wrap), then emits a classic **bordered ASCII table** — `+---+---+` rules, `| cell | cell |` rows, each cell left-padded to its column width. **`<th>` header cells are upper-cased** for emphasis (single-weight font) and set off by a separator rule under the header. It reuses the existing text pipeline: the formatted lines go through `sel_emit`/`wrap_text` like any other text (leading `+`/`|` means no leading-space is ever dropped). `<table>` is intercepted in `render_html` with a **nesting-aware** close-match so the inner `tr`/`td`/`th` aren't double-rendered. **Pinned by `formtest`, now 6/6** (was 5/5): a new case renders a `Name/Age` table with `<th>` headers and asserts the **exact** aligned lines back — `| NAME  | AGE |`, `| Alice | 30  |`, `| Bob   | 5   |`, and the rule `+-------+-----+`. **Verified live in the GUI, 0 faults** (screenshot): a host-served Planets page renders a 4-column table — **`PLANET | MOONS | DAY (H) | RINGS`** header (upper-cased, ruled off) over five aligned rows inside top/bottom borders — with normal paragraph text flowing before and after it, title + `0 links` in the status bar. **Honest scope:** no `colspan`/`rowspan`, and a table nested inside a cell renders only partially (the inner table bounds the outer cell's text); very wide tables get their widest columns truncated to fit the line. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1590.0 -> 1593.5 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** more Selene polish (tabs / multiple pages, or nested-table handling) or back to TLS refinements (fuller CA bundle, ECDSA-P384 SKE `0x0503`). (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~23 out.) |
| v5.9.76 | **Selene: `<img>` placeholders — images stop vanishing; each one renders as a framed box showing its alt text (or filename).** NyxOS has no image decoder yet, so Selene had been silently dropping every `<img>`, leaving image-carrying pages full of gaps. This parses the tag's **`alt`** and **`src`** and renders the image inline as a **framed placeholder** — a cool-slate box with a **purple "media" accent bar** — captioned **`[img: <alt>]`**, or **`[img: <filename>]`** when there's no alt (the basename of `src`), so pages read as structure instead of holes. It reuses the browser's existing overlay architecture rather than adding a new mechanism: a per-char **`img_of`** grid parallel to `link_of`/`field_of`, an `images[]` array of `{alt, src}`, threaded through `wrap_text` and painted in its own draw pass. Images get their **own budget** (`SEL_MAX_IMGS 128`) so they never starve the 64-slot form-field pool, and links / forms / find-in-page are untouched. **Pinned by `formtest`, now 5/5** (was 4/4): a new case renders `<img src="/logo.png" alt="Company Logo">` plus a no-alt `<img src="http://cdn.example/cat.jpg">` and asserts **alt captured, `src` filename fallback (`cat.jpg`), and both `[img: …]` labels present in the rendered grid**. **Verified live in the GUI, 0 faults** (screenshot): a page served to the guest over HTTP renders **`[img: NyxOS Logo]` inline inside a sentence**, **`[img: kitten.jpg]`** (filename fallback), and a **three-box gallery** (`[img: Mountain at dawn]` `[img: River valley]` `[img: Forest path]`) — all as purple-accented frames — beside a still-working blue link and the page title + `1 links` in the status bar. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1588.1 -> 1590.0 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** more Selene polish (`<table>` column layout, or tabs) or back to TLS refinements (fuller CA bundle, ECDSA-P384 SKE `0x0503`). (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~24 out.) |
| v5.9.75 | **Selene: find-in-page (Ctrl+F) — back to the browser after the long TLS arc, with a visible feature.** Press **Ctrl+F** to open a find bar; typing filters the page live, every match is highlighted, and **Enter** cycles through them (wrapping), **Esc** closes. Matching is a case-insensitive substring search over the rendered-text grid; **all matches get a yellow background and the current one an orange accent**, the find bar shows the running **`[current/total]`** count, and jumping to a match scrolls it into view. It slots into the existing render/overlay pipeline (same idea as the link/field overlays) — a per-line highlight pass plus a bar that replaces the status strip while active — and resets on navigation. **Verified live in the GUI, 0 faults** (screenshots): on example.com, **`Ctrl+F "domain"` lights up both occurrences** — "DOMAIN" in the heading and "domain" in the body — with the find bar reading **`Find: domain_  [1/2]  Enter=next  Esc=close`**; pressing **Enter moves the orange accent to the second match and the bar to `[2/2]`**. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1586.3 -> 1588.1 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** more Selene polish (tabs, `<img>` placeholders, `<table>` layout) or back to TLS refinements (fuller CA bundle, ECDSA-P384 SKE). (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~25 out.) |
| v5.9.74 | **RSA-PSS ServerKeyExchange verification + the SKE signature folded into strict mode — strict TLS is now fully MITM-safe.** Two gaps closed. **(1) RSA-PSS:** modern servers (gnu.org and a large share of the web) sign the ServerKeyExchange with **`rsa_pss_rsae_sha256` (`0x0804`)**, which NyxOS couldn't verify — it reported "not supported" and proceeded unverified. New **`rsa_pss_sha256_verify`** in `rsa.c` implements **RSASSA-PSS** (EMSA-PSS-VERIFY: `s^e mod N` via the existing modexp, then **MGF1-SHA256** unmasking, the `0x…01‖salt` check, and `H' = SHA-256(0x00×8 ‖ mHash ‖ salt)`), salt length 32. Pinned by **`rsatest`, now 5/5** — the three PKCS1 cases plus a **valid PSS signature accepted and a tampered one rejected**, against a signature from a standard library. `tls.c`'s SKE verifier dispatches `0x0804` to it (alongside ECDSA-P256 `0x0403` and RSA-PKCS1 `0x0401`). **(2) Strict now checks the SKE:** v5.9.72's strict mode gated on chain + hostname + dates but *not* the ServerKeyExchange signature, so a server whose SKE used an unverifiable scheme still passed — an active MITM could have substituted its own ECDHE parameters behind the real certificate. Strict mode now **also requires the SKE signature to be verified**. **Verified live, 0 faults:** `tls www.gnu.org` — whose SKE is RSA-PSS — now prints **`ServerKeyExchange signature VERIFIED against the leaf certificate (RSA-PSS)`** and, crucially, **passes `tlsstrict on`** (before, it either wasn't verified or slipped through strict unverified). So strict TLS now enforces the *complete* chain of trust: **chain → a bundled root, hostname/SAN, validity dates, AND the key-exchange signature** — for ECDSA-P256, RSA-PKCS1 and RSA-PSS. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1583.2 -> 1586.3 KB (all in `rsa.c` / `tls.c`). **Next:** a fuller CA bundle, ECDSA-P384 SKE (`0x0503`), or a pivot back to the Selene browser. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~26 out.) |
| v5.9.73 | **A real TLS trust store — strict mode now works across the web, not just SSL.com-rooted sites.** v5.9.72 gave NyxOS strict certificate enforcement, but with a single pinned root it was only *usable* for example.com. This bundles a small set of **root CA public keys** (`x509_roots.h`) covering both key types — **EC (P-384)** and **RSA (2048 & 4096-bit)** — and generalizes the anchor check from one hard-coded key to a **trust-store lookup**: `x509.c`'s `trusted_root_name()` walks the bundle and matches the chain's top certificate by EC point or by RSA `{N, e}` (leading-zero-normalized). The five anchors are **SSL.com TLS ECC Root CA 2022** (EC-P384), **Amazon Root CA 1** (RSA-2048), **DigiCert Global G2 CA** (RSA-2048), **GTS Root R4** (EC-P384) and the **ISRG RSA-4096** root — the anchors that example.com, httpbin.org, mojeek, w3.org/danluu and gnu.org actually present. **Verified live under `tlsstrict on`, 0 faults:** `chaintest` stays **5/5** (example.com anchors via the store), and in **strict mode** — where each site's chain must reach a trusted root — **`tls example.com`** (→ SSL.com ECC root), **`tls httpbin.org`** (→ **Amazon Root CA 1**, which strict mode *refused* one version ago), **`tls www.w3.org`** (→ GTS Root R4) and **`tls www.gnu.org`** (→ the ISRG **RSA-4096** root) all print **`certificate chain VERIFIED — anchored to trusted root: <name>`** and fetch. So the whole verification stack now admits the genuinely-trusted across EC and RSA roots. **Honest scope:** the store is **key-pinned to these specific anchors** (a full CA bundle is more of the same, just bigger); and strict mode gates on chain + hostname + dates but **not yet on the ServerKeyExchange signature**, so a server whose SKE uses an unsupported scheme (gnu.org uses **RSA-PSS `0x0804`**, still "unverified") is admitted on the other three checks. **No new syscalls** (still 57); new file **`kernel/x509_roots.h`** (git add); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1581.2 -> 1583.2 KB. **Next:** RSA-PSS SKE verification (and folding the SKE result into strict), a fuller root bundle, or browser polish. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~27 out.) |
| v5.9.72 | **TLS certificate enforcement — the trust model can now REFUSE a connection, not just warn (MITM defence goes from "reported" to "blocked").** Everything NyxOS verifies — the chain to a pinned root (v5.9.65), the leaf hostname/SAN + validity dates (v5.9.66), and the ServerKeyExchange signature for ECDSA + RSA (v5.9.61/71) — was until now **reported and then allowed to proceed**, because with a single pinned trust anchor, hard-failing everything that doesn't chain to it would make most of the web unreachable. This adds a **strict mode** so enforcement is real when you want it, without breaking default browsing. New **`tlsstrict [on\|off]`** command toggles it (off by default). When **on**, `tls_https_request` **refuses the handshake** unless ALL of: the chain anchors to a pinned root, the hostname is covered by the leaf's subjectAltName, and the certificate is within its validity dates. (A **forged chain link or an invalid SKE signature is refused either way** — that was never optional.) **Verified live, 0 faults:** with strict **off**, `tls httpbin.org` prints "proceeding without a pinned anchor" and **fetches**; after **`tlsstrict on`**, the same `tls httpbin.org` prints **`STRICT MODE — refusing httpbin.org (not anchored to a trusted root)`** and **aborts** — while `tls example.com`, which *is* anchored to the pinned SSL.com root and matches host + dates, **still fetches under strict mode**. So strict mode blocks the untrusted and admits the fully-verified — the whole verification stack now works as a real gate. **Honest scope:** with one pinned root, strict mode is currently only *usable* for SSL.com-rooted sites (e.g. example.com); a broader **trust store** is what makes strict mode practical for everyday browsing — the natural next step. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1580.1 -> 1581.2 KB (all in `tls.c`). **Next: a broader trust store** (bundle more roots) so strict mode covers most of the web, then RSA-PSS SKE / other refinements. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~28 out.) |
| v5.9.71 | **TLS now verifies the RSA-signed ServerKeyExchange too — ECDHE-RSA servers are no longer trusted "unverified".** Since v5.9.61 the SKE-signature check only handled **ECDSA-P256** (sig alg `0x0403`), so every ECDHE-**RSA** server (httpbin, and a large share of the web) reached "proceeding unverified". This adds the RSA path, reusing `rsa.c` (v5.9.62) + `der_x509_pubkey`'s RSA extraction (v5.9.65). The SKE verify is refactored to **dispatch by signature algorithm**: it computes `e = SHA-256(client_random ‖ server_random ‖ ServerECDHParams)` once, then verifies **ECDSA-P256 (`0x0403`)** against the leaf's EC key or **RSA-PKCS1-v1.5 / SHA-256 (`0x0401`)** against the leaf's RSA `{N, e}` (`rsa_pkcs1_sha256_verify`). **A real bug the live test caught:** the SKE-signature buffer was `tls_ske_sig[128]`, big enough for a ~70-byte ECDSA signature but **too small for a 256-byte RSA-2048 signature** — so RSA sigs were silently never captured (`ske_sig_len` stayed 0) and the code reported the algorithm as "not supported" for what was actually `0x0401`. Enlarged to **512 bytes** (fits RSA up to 4096-bit). **Verified live, 0 faults:** **`tls example.com`** still prints **`VERIFIED … (ECDSA-P256)`** (regression), and **`tls httpbin.org`** — an ECDHE-RSA server — now prints **`ServerKeyExchange signature VERIFIED against the leaf certificate (RSA)`** and fetches. **Honest scope:** the two dominant TLS-1.2 SKE schemes (ECDSA-P256/SHA-256 + RSA-PKCS1/SHA-256) are covered; the rarer ones NyxOS also offers — RSA-PSS (`0x0804`), RSA-PKCS1-SHA512 (`0x0601`), ECDSA-P384 (`0x0503`) — still "proceed unverified". **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1579.9 -> 1580.1 KB. **Next:** TLS enforcement (hard-fail wrong-host / expired / unanchored), a broader trust store, or verifying the certificate-chain links' RSA-PSS signatures. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~29 out.) |
| v5.9.70 | **NIST P-256 (secp256r1) ECDHE key agreement — NyxOS's TLS now reaches the huge set of servers that prefer P-256, not just x25519 ones.** Until now the handshake dead-ended whenever a server chose curve `0x17` (P-256) for the ephemeral key exchange (v5.9.69's finding — example.com/mojeek prefer x25519 and worked; httpbin/postman-echo prefer P-256 and failed). This adds the P-256 side, reusing `p256.c`'s point arithmetic. The ECDHE block in `tls.c` is refactored to be **curve-generic**: it computes the per-curve `(ephemeral_public, pre_master)` and then shares the key schedule, Finished exchange and fetch. For **P-256**: a CSPRNG ephemeral scalar `d`, our public point **`d·G`** (`p256_base_scalar` → the 65-byte `0x04‖X‖Y`), and the **pre-master = the X-coordinate of `d·serverPubkey`** (`p256_point_scalar`). The **ClientKeyExchange** is now variable-length (32-byte x25519 point or 65-byte P-256 point). **Verified live, 0 faults:** **`tls example.com`** still prints **`ECDHE(x25519)`** and fetches (x25519 regression intact), and **`tls httpbin.org`** — which NyxOS could NOT handshake with before — now prints **`ECDHE(secp256r1 (P-256))`** and **completes the handshake + fetches the page**. That the server Finished verifies is end-to-end proof the ECDH shared secret is computed correctly. **Bonus — this unblocked the https-POST test that v5.9.69 couldn't run:** **`posttest`** now POSTs over **TLS** to `https://httpbin.org/post` and gets **`HTTPS 200 OK`** with the **server echoing the posted form fields back** — so the TLS POST path is now proven live too, not just by composition. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1579.3 -> 1579.9 KB (all in `tls.c` — the curve math was already there). **Next:** verify the RSA-signed ServerKeyExchange against RSA leaf certs (ECDHE-RSA servers currently proceed unverified), TLS enforcement (hard-fail wrong-host/expired), or a broader trust store. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~30 out.) |
| v5.9.69 | **POST forms — Selene can submit login-style forms (the complement to GET).** The request layer learned to send a body: **`http_request`** (http.c) and **`tls_https_request`** (tls.c) take a `method` + optional form body and emit `Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded` + `Content-Length` followed by the data (`http_get` / `tls_https_fetch` are now thin GET wrappers). Selene's `selene_load_ex(method, body)` drives either over http or TLS, and **`selene_submit`** now branches on the form's method: a **GET** form navigates to `action?query` (as before), a **`method=post`** form sends the same URL-encoded `name=value&…` as the **request body** to the action and renders the reply. The query/body builder (`selene_build_query`) is shared, so GET and POST encode identically. **Verified two ways, 0 faults:** **`formtest` is now 4/4** — the three GET cases plus a **POST form** whose body builds to **`user=alice&pass=p%40ss+w`** (`@`→`%40`, space→`+`); and a new **`posttest`** command does a **live HTTP POST to httpbin.org** and confirms **`HTTP 200 OK`** with **the server echoing back the posted fields** (`nyxos`, `works`, `hello world`). **Honest scope + a finding:** the live POST test runs over **plain HTTP** because the public POST-echo services (httpbin, postman-echo) negotiate **P-256 (secp256r1) ECDHE**, and **NyxOS only implements x25519 key agreement** — so their TLS handshake never completes (example.com and mojeek happen to prefer x25519, which is why *those* work). The **TLS POST path is the same request bytes sealed in a record** — the sealing/receiving is already proven by the GET fetches — but a *direct https-POST* live test is blocked until NyxOS speaks **P-256 ECDHE** (a natural next step: `p256.c` already has the point arithmetic). The TLS POST body is bounded to ~512 bytes (one record; login forms fit). **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1576.2 -> 1579.3 KB. **Next: P-256 ECDHE key agreement** (reach the many servers that prefer secp256r1 — the biggest reach-widener now), then enforcement / a broader trust store. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~31 out.) |
| v5.9.68 | **HTML forms in Selene — the browser payoff TLS was gating all along (search boxes work).** Selene's HTML parser now understands **`<form>`** (its `action` + `method`), **`<input>`** (text / search / password → an editable box; `submit` → a button; `hidden` → carried silently), and **`<button>`** (submit-style). Controls render **inline** as focusable widgets via a new per-character **`field_of` grid** (the same trick as the existing `link_of` link grid): text inputs draw as bordered boxes showing their live value with a caret when focused, submit controls as filled buttons. **Tab** cycles focus in **document order** across links *and* fields (so a search box is reachable, not stranded after every link), a **click** focuses a text box or presses a button, **typing** edits the focused field, and **Enter / clicking submit** builds the GET query — `action?name=value&...` with proper **URL-encoding** (space→`+`, unreserved kept, else `%XX`), hidden fields included — and navigates. **Verified two ways:** a new **`formtest`** command runs a self-test that parses a known form and builds its submission URL — **3/3 passed, 0 faults** (form parsed as GET with the resolved action; fields parsed as text `q` + hidden `lang=en` + submit; `q="hello world"` → **`http://example.com/search?q=hello+world&lang=en`**); and **live in the GUI**, Selene fetched **https://www.mojeek.com** (a real search engine, 200 OK, 33 links) over TLS and **rendered its search form** — a text input box and a submit button — captured in a screenshot. **Two rendering fixes fell out of the visual test:** focus now follows **document order** (it was links-then-fields, which buried the search box), and the text box uses a **light-grey fill with a darker border** (it was white-on-near-white and invisible). **Honest scope:** **GET only** — a `method=post` form currently still submits as a GET query (real POST needs a request body + `Content-Type`, a later step); `<input>` types beyond text/submit/hidden (checkbox, radio, file, …) are skipped; single-word submit labels render cleanest. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1568.1 -> 1576.2 KB (all in `selene_win.c`). **Next:** **POST** (login forms), or trust-store / enforcement hardening. (Reminder: v5.9.100 begins a general maintenance pass — ~32 versions out.) |
| v5.9.67 | **Streaming https receive — Selene can now load large pages (back to the browser after the crypto arc).** The response path used to read the *entire encrypted reply into the 16 KB `tls_rx` buffer* before decrypting, so any https page over ~16 KB was silently truncated. `tls_https_fetch` now **streams**: it decrypts each complete TLS record as it arrives, appends the plaintext into the caller's buffer, and **compacts `tls_rx`** (sliding the leftover partial record to the front). The page size is now bounded only by the **caller's buffer** — Selene already passes a 64 KB heap buffer (`HTTP_MAX_RESPONSE`), so pages up to 64 KB load whole. `tls_rx` grew 16384 -> 20480 (it now only has to hold **one** TLS record at a time, and a maxed record is ~16.4 KB), with a guard that rejects an illegally oversize record and a bump of the transfer hard-cap to 20 s. The `tls` diagnostic command now allocates the same 64 KB buffer (was the 16 KB `tls_hd`) and reports the total decrypted length. **Verified live, 0 faults:** **`tls example.com`** still fetches its 868-byte page with the chain **VERIFIED** (regression intact), and **`tls www.w3.org`** — TLS 1.2 + x25519 + ECDHE-ECDSA-AES128-GCM, a **54 612-byte** page across several TLS records — now decrypts **in full** (`total decrypted response = 54612 bytes`), where the old 16 KB buffer would have cut it off at ~16 KB. (w3.org's chain roots outside NyxOS's single pinned anchor, so it's reported "not a pinned trusted root" and proceeds — same honest single-anchor stance.) **No new files, no new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1567.8 -> 1568.1 KB. **Next:** HTML **forms** (GET/POST) — the feature TLS was gating all along — and/or trust-store/enforcement hardening. (Reminder: v5.9.100 kicks off a general maintenance pass.) |
| v5.9.66 | **Leaf-certificate hostname + validity checks — "verify the peer" is now complete.** The chain walk (v5.9.65) proved the certificate is *authentic*; this adds the two checks that prove it's the *right* certificate for *this* connection, *right now*. **Hostname:** a new `der_x509_extension` finds the **subjectAltName** extension and `x509_check_host` matches the requested host against its **dNSName** entries — case-insensitively, honouring a single leading **`*.` wildcard** (one label: `*.example.com` matches `www.example.com` but not `example.com` or `a.b.example.com`). **Validity:** `der_x509_validity` parses **notBefore/notAfter** (both **UTCTime** `YYMMDDHHMMSSZ` and **GeneralizedTime** `YYYYMMDDHHMMSSZ`, with RFC 5280's 2000/1900 sliding window) into a comparable packed `YYYYMMDDHHMMSS`, and `x509_check_validity` compares them against the **RTC** (`rtc_read_time`). `tls.c` runs both on the leaf after the chain verifies. **Verified two ways, 0 faults:** **`chaintest` now 5/5** — the three chain tests plus **hostname (SAN) match** (`example.com` exact + `www.example.com` via the wildcard accepted; `attacker.com`, `example.org`, and `a.b.example.com` rejected) and **validity-date parsing** (the real leaf's `nb=20260531213912 na=20260829214126`); and **live `tls example.com`** printed **`leaf certificate covers host 'example.com' (subjectAltName match)`** and **`leaf certificate is within its validity period`** alongside the chain + fetch — the RTC clock (≈2026-07-26) correctly falls inside the leaf's 2026-05-31 … 2026-08-29 window. **Honest scope:** these are **reported, not enforced** — a wrong-host or expired certificate is flagged loudly but NyxOS still proceeds (same single-anchor stance as the chain check; hard-fail enforcement is a policy choice for later). No SAN → host not checked (legacy CN fallback not implemented; modern certs always carry SAN). **No new files, no new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1564.2 -> 1567.8 KB (all in der.c / x509.c / tls.c). The TLS peer-verification story is now whole: **authentic (chain to a pinned root) + correct host + in date.** **Next:** enforcement policy (hard-fail on mismatch), a broader trust store, or back to the browser — grow the ~16 KB `tls_rx` for large https pages and add HTML forms (GET/POST). |
| v5.9.65 | **TLS certificate-chain verification to a pinned trust anchor — the trust model is closed, TLS is now MITM-resistant (FINALE of the crypto arc).** Everything before this proved the server holds the key matching its *leaf* certificate (the ServerKeyExchange signature, v5.9.61) — but not that the certificate is *trustworthy*; a man-in-the-middle with its own self-consistent certificate still passed. This closes that gap. New **`x509.c`** walks the certificate chain the server sends and verifies **each certificate's signature under the next one's public key**, dispatching by the `signatureAlgorithm` OID to **ECDSA-P256/SHA-256**, **ECDSA-P384/SHA-384**, or **RSA-PKCS1/SHA-256** — every primitive built across v5.9.49–64 meeting at once — and requires the **topmost certificate to carry a root public key pinned into the kernel** (the **SSL.com TLS ECC Root CA 2022**, P-384; a root's key *is* its identity, so it needs no signature of its own). A broken link (bad signature) means tampering and **aborts the handshake**; only a chain that reaches the pinned anchor is fully trusted. Four new **DER helpers** (`der_x509_tbs` — the exact signed bytes; `der_x509_sig_alg`; `der_x509_signature`; `der_x509_pubkey` — EC-curve/RSA classification) feed the walk. `tls.c` now records the whole chain (not just the leaf) and runs the verification after the flight-1 parse. **Design proven in Python first** (manual `tbsCertificate` extraction cross-checked against `cryptography`'s own, all three real links verified) before a line of C. **Verified two ways, 0 faults:** **`chaintest` 3/3** — a real captured 4-cert chain is **TRUSTED** (anchored to the pinned root), a **one-byte-tampered leaf is REJECTED** as forged, and a **root-less prefix is refused** (top isn't a pinned root); and **live `tls example.com`** printed **`certificate chain VERIFIED — anchored to the pinned SSL.com TLS ECC Root CA 2022 (3 links)`** then completed the handshake and fetched the page — NyxOS parsed example.com's real chain (leaf ECDSA-P256/SHA-256 ← Cloudflare ECC CA3 ← SSL.com Transit, both ECDSA-P384/SHA-384 ← the pinned root) end to end. **Honest scope:** NyxOS ships a **single** trust anchor, so only chains reaching the SSL.com ECC root are *fully* trusted; other sites still load (the chain's internal links are checked — a *tampered* chain is always caught — but they're reported as not-anchored rather than refused, since a one-root store can't verify most of the web). Validity dates (`notBefore`/`notAfter`) and hostname/SAN matching are the remaining refinements. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1553.8 -> 1564.2 KB (`x509.o` + the embedded real-chain test vector). This completes the TLS trust arc: **NyxOS speaks TLS 1.2 end to end AND verifies the peer it's talking to.** **Next:** dates + hostname checks, a broader trust store, then back up to the browser — grow the ~16 KB `tls_rx` for large https pages and add forms (GET/POST). |
| v5.9.64 | **NIST P-384 (secp384r1) curve arithmetic + ECDSA-P384/SHA-384 verify — the other primitive the CA links need.** With SHA-384 in place (v5.9.63), this adds the curve those signatures live on. New **`p384.c`** is `p256.c` one size up: **GF(p) arithmetic over 6x64-bit limbs** (Montgomery multiplication via the same CIOS `mont_core`, now generalized to any modulus + its `n0`; native `__int128` limb products), the P-384 prime `p = 2^384 - 2^128 - 2^96 + 2^32 - 1`, point **double / add / scalar-multiply in Jacobian coordinates** (`a = -3`), affine I/O, and **`p384_ecdsa_verify(Q, hash, r, s)`** with full scalar arithmetic **mod the group order `n`** (`w = s^-1`, `u1 = e*w`, `u2 = r*w`, `R = u1*G + u2*Q`, accept iff `R.x mod n == r`). Every constant (`R^2 mod p`, `R^2 mod n`, both `n0`, the base point) was computed in Python, never recalled. Exposed as **`p384test`**. **Verified live in-guest** — `p384test` printed **4/4 passed, 0 faults**: `2*G`, `3*G` and a **384-bit scalar** `k*G` all matching NIST base-point multiples, plus **ECDSA-P384/SHA-384 verify** accepting a genuine signature (produced and independently verified by a standard library) and **rejecting a one-bit-tampered hash**. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1544.6 -> 1553.8 KB (`p384.o`). NyxOS now has **every** signature primitive a real certificate chain uses: **ECDSA-P256, ECDSA-P384, and RSA**, over **SHA-256 and SHA-384**. **Next (v5.9.65) — the chain walk, the final step:** save pointers to every certificate in the handshake, verify each link's signature under its issuer's public key (dispatching by the `signatureAlgorithm` OID to ECDSA-P256/P384 or RSA over the SHA-256/384 of its `tbsCertificate`) up to a **bundled root-CA trust anchor**, and check validity dates + the hostname. That closes the man-in-the-middle gap and makes TLS genuinely trustworthy. |
| v5.9.63 | **SHA-512 / SHA-384 — the hash the certificate chain's CA links need.** Inspecting example.com's real chain (4 certs) revealed the CA links are signed with **ECDSA-P384 / SHA-384**, which the kernel couldn't do — so verifying a chain up to a trusted root needs SHA-384 and P-384 first. This is SHA-384 (and its parent SHA-512): new **`sha512.c`** implements FIPS 180-4 SHA-512 (64-bit words, 80 rounds, 128-byte blocks, 128-bit length field) with SHA-384 as the same core under a different initial state and a truncated 48-byte output; both incremental (`sha512_init` / `sha384_init` / `update` / `final`) and one-shot APIs. Exposed as **`sha512test`**. **Verified live in-guest** — `sha512test` printed **5/5 passed, 0 faults**: SHA-512 and SHA-384 of `"abc"`, the empty string, and a **168-byte multi-block** message, all matching the FIPS vectors. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1539.2 -> 1544.6 KB (`sha512.o`). **Next (v5.9.64): P-384 (secp384r1)** curve arithmetic + ECDSA-P384 verify — the other primitive the CA links need. Then (v5.9.65) the chain walk to a bundled root, which finally closes the trust model. |
| v5.9.62 | **RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 signature verification — the last crypto primitive for the trust chain.** Real certificate chains almost always have an RSA root or intermediate CA, so verifying a chain up to a trusted anchor needs RSA signature verification alongside ECDSA. New **`rsa.c`** implements **RSA-PKCS1-v1.5 (SHA-256) verify**: a variable-length big-integer **modular exponentiation** `s^e mod N` (schoolbook double-and-add modular multiply over moduli up to 4096-bit — no Montgomery constants, since a verify's exponent `e = 65537` is tiny and cheap) followed by the **PKCS#1 v1.5 unpadding** (`00 01 FF…FF 00`, the SHA-256 DigestInfo prefix, then the hash) and a constant-length compare. Exposed as **`rsatest`**. **Verified live in-guest** — `rsatest` printed **3/3 passed, 0 faults**: a genuine **RSA-2048** signature produced by a standard library is **accepted**, and both a **tampered hash** and a **tampered signature** are **rejected**. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1534.8 -> 1539.2 KB (`rsa.o`). NyxOS now has every signature primitive a TLS certificate chain needs (**ECDSA-P256 + RSA**). **Next (v5.9.63) — the chain itself:** save pointers to every certificate in the handshake, verify each cert's signature under its issuer's public key (ECDSA or RSA over the SHA-256 of its `tbsCertificate`) up to a **bundled root-CA trust anchor**, and check validity dates + the hostname. That closes the man-in-the-middle gap and makes TLS genuinely trustworthy. |
| v5.9.61 | **TLS now cryptographically verifies the server's ServerKeyExchange signature against its certificate.** All the pieces (DER reader, P-256, ECDSA verify) meet the handshake. During the flight-1 parse `tls_https_fetch` now keeps a pointer to the **leaf certificate** and captures both the **ServerECDHParams** (the signed data) and the **ECDSA signature** from the ServerKeyExchange. After the key agreement it: pulls the server's **EC public key** out of the leaf cert (`der_x509_ec_pubkey`), parses the signature's DER `SEQUENCE { r, s }` (normalising each INTEGER to 32 bytes), computes `e = SHA-256(client_random ‖ server_random ‖ ServerECDHParams)`, and runs **`p256_ecdsa_verify`**. An **invalid** signature **aborts the handshake**; a cert whose key isn't P-256 is reported and skipped (for now). **Verified live in-guest against the real internet** — `tls example.com` printed **`ServerKeyExchange signature VERIFIED against the leaf certificate (ECDSA-P256)`** and then completed the handshake and fetched the page, **0 faults**: NyxOS parsed example.com's real 1003-byte leaf certificate, extracted its P-256 key, and confirmed the ECDSA signature over the real handshake parameters. **Honest scope:** this proves the ECDHE parameters were signed by the key in the presented certificate — it does **not** yet verify that the certificate chains to a trusted root, so an active MITM with its own valid certificate would still pass. That **trust-anchor / chain** check is the next (and final core) step. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1533.3 -> 1534.8 KB (all in `tls.c`). **Next (v5.9.62):** embed a root-CA trust anchor and verify the certificate chain up to it. |
| v5.9.60 | **ECDSA-P256 signature verification — the check at the heart of certificate validation.** With the curve arithmetic in place (v5.9.59), this adds the verify itself. `p256.c` gains **scalar arithmetic mod `n`** (the group order) — Montgomery multiplication (the mod-p multiplier generalized to take any modulus + its `n0`), inverse via Fermat, and range checks — and **`p256_ecdsa_verify(Q, hash, r, s)`**: it rejects out-of-range `r`/`s`, computes `w = s⁻¹ mod n`, `u1 = e·w`, `u2 = r·w`, forms `R = u1·G + u2·Q` (two scalar-mults + a Jacobian add), and accepts iff `R.x mod n == r`. **Verified live in-guest** — `p256test` now prints **4/4 passed, 0 faults**, the new line being **ECDSA-P256 verify PASS: a valid signature is accepted and a one-bit-tampered hash is rejected**, checked against a signature produced (and independently verified) by a standard library. **Tooling note:** the platform's command-approval service was briefly down mid-increment; rather than write crypto blind, the work paused and resumed once it recovered, keeping the build-and-verify-every-step discipline intact. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1530.5 -> 1533.3 KB (all in `p256.c`). **Next:** wire it into the handshake — extract the leaf certificate's public key (`der_x509_ec_pubkey`), parse the ServerKeyExchange's ECDSA signature, hash `client_random || server_random || ServerECDHParams`, and `p256_ecdsa_verify` it, rejecting the connection on failure; then chain the leaf to a bundled trust anchor. |
| v5.9.59 | **NIST P-256 (secp256r1) curve arithmetic — the math behind the certificate's signature.** The server's certificate (cipher 0xc02b) signs with **ECDSA-P256**, so verifying it needs point arithmetic on that curve. New **`p256.c`** provides the full field and group: **GF(p) via Montgomery multiplication** (4×64-bit limbs, native `__int128` products, CIOS reduction, and the constant `n0'`=1 this prime happens to have) with add / sub / mul / square / inverse, and **point add / double / scalar-multiply in Jacobian coordinates** for the `a = -3` short-Weierstrass form, with affine byte I/O and identity / equal / opposite-point handling. Exposed as **`p256test`**. **Verified live in-guest** — `p256test` printed **3/3 passed, 0 faults**: `2·G`, `3·G`, and a full **256-bit scalar × G**, each byte-identical to the point an independent library computes (so the Montgomery field, the Jacobian doubling and addition, the double-and-add ladder, and the Montgomery→affine conversion are all correct). **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1523.4 -> 1530.5 KB (`p256.o`). **Next in the cert arc:** **ECDSA-P256 verify** — the mod-`n` scalar side (`s⁻¹`, `u1 = e·s⁻¹`, `u2 = r·s⁻¹`) plus the check `x-coord(u1·G + u2·Q) ≡ r (mod n)`, KAT'd against a known signature; then wire it into the handshake to verify the **ServerKeyExchange** signature under the certificate's key, and finally chain to a bundled trust anchor. |
| v5.9.58 | **DER/ASN.1 reader — reading an X.509 certificate, step 1 of the TLS trust model.** TLS works and is now unpredictably-keyed (v5.9.57), but it still trusts *any* certificate — an active man-in-the-middle could present its own. Closing that means verifying the server's certificate, which is **DER-encoded** (nested Tag-Length-Value). New **`der.c`** is a small, fully bounds-checked DER cursor — `der_read` (short and **long-form** lengths), `der_enter` (descend into a SEQUENCE / SET / context tag), `der_skip` — plus one navigator, **`der_x509_ec_pubkey`**, that walks `Certificate → tbsCertificate → subjectPublicKeyInfo → subjectPublicKey` (stepping over the optional `[0]` version, serial, signature, issuer, validity and subject) and returns the server's raw EC public-key point (`0x04 || X || Y`). Exposed as **`dertest`**. **Verified live in-guest** — `dertest` printed **3/3 passed, 0 faults**: TLV parsing + nesting, a long-form length (a 256-byte value behind a `0x82 0x01 0x00` length), and **extracting the 65-byte EC point from a real self-signed P-256 certificate**, byte-identical to the point an independent library reports. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1520.0 -> 1523.4 KB (`der.o`). **Next in the cert-verification arc:** **P-256** field + point arithmetic (secp256r1), then **ECDSA-P256 verify**, then use them to check the **ServerKeyExchange signature** under the certificate's key (example.com is ECDSA-P256, cipher 0xc02b), and finally a chain to a bundled **trust anchor**. |
| v5.9.57 | **A real CSPRNG — the TLS ephemeral key and ClientHello random are now unpredictable.** TLS was functional but not yet *secure*: the ClientHello random and — critically — the **ephemeral X25519 private key** came from a plain LCG, so a passive observer could recompute the "secret" and decrypt the session. New **`csprng.c`** replaces it with a proper **HMAC_DRBG-SHA256 (NIST SP 800-90A)** built on the existing HMAC-SHA256, **seeded from hardware entropy** — **RDSEED** then **RDRAND** when the CPU exposes them (detected via CPUID), always XOR-folded with **RDTSC** jitter and the tick counter, re-stirred with a fresh timing sample on every draw plus a periodic reseed. `csprng_bytes(out, n)` lazily instantiates on first use. **Wired into `tls.c`:** the ClientHello random and the ephemeral private key now come from `csprng_bytes` (the LCG `tls_rand_byte` is gone). Exposed as a **`csprngtest`** command; the DRBG core is pinned by a **NIST-format known-answer test** (cross-checked against an independent HMAC_DRBG implementation). **Verified live in-guest** (booted `-cpu qemu64,+rdrand,+rdseed`): `csprngtest` printed **the NIST HMAC_DRBG vector PASS**, **RDRAND=yes / RDSEED=yes** (hardware entropy detected and used), and `tls example.com` still **completed the handshake and fetched the page** with the CSPRNG-generated key — **0 faults** (the `rdrand`/`rdseed` instructions are guarded behind CPUID, so a CPU without them cleanly falls back to RDTSC + ticks). **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1516.9 -> 1520.0 KB (`csprng.o`). **Next:** **certificate/signature verification** (X.509 parse of the leaf cert + validate the ServerKeyExchange signature against a trust anchor) — the last big piece of the TLS trust model; then a larger TLS receive buffer for big pages, and forms (GET/POST). |
| v5.9.56 | **Selene opens https sites — the browser speaks TLS.** The user-facing payoff of the whole TLS arc: an `https://` URL now takes the real TLS path instead of being downgraded to plain `:80`. **Refactor:** the v5.9.55 fetch-and-decrypt core became a reusable **`tls_https_fetch(host, path, iface, out, cap, verbose)`** (the `tls` command is now a thin wrapper over it), and `http_get`'s response parsing was extracted into **`http_parse_response()`** so the https path parses the decrypted reply exactly like http — status line, `Content-Length`, and **chunked** de-framing. **Selene wiring:** `parse_url` detects the scheme (https → port 443), `selene_load` routes https through `tls_https_fetch` + `http_parse_response`, and `selene_resolve` is now **scheme-aware** — it keeps `https://` links (the old code force-downgraded them to http) and resolves relative links against the page's own scheme, so you can browse *within* a secure site. **A real bug, caught by the browser and not by the command.** After the refactor the `tls` command still printed the page, yet Selene showed only "Bad https response". A local **`uint8_t out[128]`** (the second-flight record buffer) **shadowed the new `out` parameter**, so the record decryptor wrote the 868-byte response into a 128-byte *stack* array — a silent overflow — instead of the caller's buffer. The command masked it by reading that same stack data straight back; Selene read its own (untouched) heap buffer and saw nothing. Renaming the local to `f2` fixed both the correctness *and* the overflow. Pinned by instrumenting the pointer: `out=0x27d3c0` inside the fetch vs `raw=0x64aff8` in the caller. **Verified live in-guest:** the `tls example.com` command still fetches (200 OK, server Finished verified), and **Selene loads `https://example.com` over TLS and renders it** — a screenshot shows the **EXAMPLE DOMAIN** heading, the body paragraph, the blue **Learn more** link, and a **`200 OK - Example Domain - 1 links`** status bar. **0 faults.** **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1516.0 -> 1516.9 KB. **Next:** a **CSPRNG** for the ephemeral key + ClientHello random (replacing the LCG), then **certificate/signature verification** (X.509) for the trust model; larger pages will also want the ~16 KB TLS receive buffer grown. |
| v5.9.55 | **https, for real — NyxOS fetches and decrypts a live https page over the TLS channel.** The payoff of the whole crypto arc: over the secure channel v5.9.54 established, `tls_hello` now sends an **encrypted HTTP GET** and **decrypts the reply**. It seals `GET / HTTP/1.1\r\nHost: <host>\r\nConnection: close…` as an **application-data record** (type 0x17) at **client sequence 1** (the client Finished was seq 0), sends it, then reads the server's application-data records and **`tls_open_record`s each** at **server sequence 1, 2, …** (the server Finished was its seq 0), concatenating the decrypted plaintext and stopping at the encrypted close_notify. The per-direction sequence-number bookkeeping is the crux, and it lines up. **Verified live in-guest against the real internet** — `tls example.com` completed the handshake and then printed **`decrypted 868 bytes of https response`** followed by the real page: **`HTTP/1.1 200 OK`** … **`<title>Example Domain</title>`**, then **`*** https fetch over TLS succeeded ***`**, **0 faults**. NyxOS reads the encrypted web end to end. **Known limits (printed in the output):** the ephemeral key still uses a **non-crypto RNG** and the **server certificate is not verified** — a working https *client*, not yet a trusting one. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1514.7 -> 1516.0 KB (all in `tls.c`). **Next:** wire **`https`** into `http_get` / Selene so an `https://` URL takes this TLS path instead of plain `:80` (the browser can then open secure sites), then a **CSPRNG** for the ephemeral key and **certificate/signature verification** for the trust model. |
| v5.9.54 | **TLS, step 7 — the handshake COMPLETES: NyxOS establishes a verified, encrypted TLS channel with a real https server.** The milestone the whole arc built toward. `tls_hello` now finishes the handshake instead of stopping after the key schedule: it accumulates the **handshake transcript** (every handshake-layer message — ClientHello, the server's ServerHello…ServerHelloDone, our ClientKeyExchange), sends its **second flight** — **ClientKeyExchange** (our ephemeral X25519 public key), **ChangeCipherSpec**, and an **encrypted Finished** (the record layer sealing `verify_data = PRF(master, "client finished", SHA256(transcript))`) — then reads the server's **ChangeCipherSpec + encrypted Finished**, GCM-**opens** it, and checks its `verify_data` against `PRF(master, "server finished", …)`. **A matching server Finished is the definitive end-to-end proof:** the server independently derived the same value from its own view of the transcript and keys, so it confirms *every* layer at once — the X25519 agreement, the key schedule, the transcript, the PRF labels, and the AES-128-GCM record framing. The function was restructured to keep the TCP connection open across both flights (every exit path now closes it). **Verified live in-guest against the real internet** — `tls example.com` printed the whole flow and then **`*** HANDSHAKE COMPLETE — server Finished verified; secure channel established with example.com ***`**, **0 faults**: the real example.com server accepted our Finished, and its own Finished decrypted and matched. NyxOS now speaks TLS 1.2 end to end. **Known limits (printed in the output):** the ephemeral key still uses a **non-crypto RNG** and the **certificate is not verified** yet — so this proves the protocol, not yet the trust model. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1513.6 -> 1514.7 KB (all in `tls.c`). **Next:** send an encrypted **https GET** over this channel and **decrypt the application-data reply** → a real https page; then wire `https` into `http_get`/Selene, and later a CSPRNG + certificate/signature verification. |
| v5.9.53 | **TLS, step 6 — the record layer: AES-128-GCM records + the Finished verify_data, framed exactly on the wire.** With all four crypto primitives in hand, this builds the two pieces the Finished exchange rides on and pins them with offline vectors before the live handshake depends on them. **Record protection (RFC 5288):** `tls_seal_record` / `tls_open_record` wrap AES-128-GCM in the TLS 1.2 framing — the 12-byte **nonce** is `write_IV(4) || explicit_nonce(8)`, the 13-byte **AAD** is `seq(8) || type || version || length`, and the on-wire fragment is `explicit_nonce(8) || ciphertext || tag(16)` (the 64-bit sequence number is sent as the explicit nonce). Open **verifies the tag before returning any plaintext**. **Finished:** `tls_verify_data` computes `PRF(master_secret, "client finished" | "server finished", SHA256(handshake_transcript))[0..11]` — the value each side proves it holds so both confirm nothing in the handshake was altered. Exposed as **`tlsrectest`**; the expected record bytes and verify_data were generated by independent implementations. **Verified live in-guest** — `tlsrectest` printed **5/5 checks, 0 faults**: a record **sealed to the exact wire bytes** an independent AES-GCM produced, **opened back** to its plaintext, a **tampered record rejected**, and **both the client and server `verify_data`** matching an independent PRF over the transcript hash. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1511.0 -> 1513.6 KB (all in `tls.c`, on the existing `aes_gcm` / `sha256` / `tls_prf`). **Next (v5.9.54): the live Finished exchange** — track the handshake transcript, send **ClientKeyExchange + ChangeCipherSpec + (encrypted) Finished**, and **verify the server's Finished** against example.com. A matching server Finished is the definitive end-to-end proof that the whole handshake is correct; after it, decrypt application-data → `https` in `http_get`/Selene. |
| v5.9.52 | **TLS, step 5 — AES-128-GCM, the record cipher: authenticated encryption that reproduces the NIST vectors byte-for-byte.** The suite example.com negotiated (0xc02b) protects every application byte with **AES-128-GCM**, so this is the last big primitive before NyxOS can read an https page. New **`aes_gcm.c`** implements **AES-128** (FIPS-197, encryption only — GCM never needs the inverse cipher) and **GCM** (NIST SP 800-38D): **CTR-mode** encryption plus a **GHASH** over **GF(2^128)** for the authentication tag, using the 96-bit-IV `J0` construction TLS uses and the hash subkey `H = E_K(0)`. Decryption **verifies the tag before releasing any plaintext** (constant-time compare) and refuses on a mismatch. It is standalone this step — wiring it into the record layer + the Finished exchange is next. Exposed as a **`gcmtest`** command; the expected outputs were produced by an independent AES-GCM implementation so every byte is certain. **Verified live in-guest** — `gcmtest` printed **5/5 cases passed, 0 faults**: the **FIPS-197** AES-128 block vector, the **McGrew-Viega GCM** test cases **2, 3 and 4** (empty-AAD single block; four full blocks; partial blocks *with* AAD — each **encrypted to the exact ciphertext+tag AND decrypted back**), and a **tamper-detection** check where a single flipped tag bit is correctly **rejected** (proving the AEAD actually authenticates, not just encrypts). **Not constant-time** (table S-box, bitwise GF multiply) — fine to prove the mechanism; a hardening pass rides with the CSPRNG work. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1504.6 -> 1511.0 KB (`aes_gcm.o`). **Next TLS steps:** send **ClientKeyExchange + ChangeCipherSpec + Finished** (encrypt the Finished under these GCM keys and verify the server's) → **decrypt application-data** records → then `https` in `http_get`/Selene. Then a CSPRNG for the ephemeral key + certificate/signature verification. |
| v5.9.51 | **TLS, step 4 — the real ECDHE key agreement: NyxOS and a live https server derive the same session keys.** This is where the two primitives meet the wire. The handshake now **saves our ClientHello random**, **parses the ServerHello random** and the **ServerKeyExchange** (the ECDHE `ServerECDHParams` — curve type, named curve, and the server's ephemeral public-key point), generates **our ephemeral X25519 keypair**, computes `x25519(our_priv, server_pub)` = the **pre-master secret**, and runs the **TLS 1.2 key schedule**: `master = PRF(pre_master, "master secret", client_random+server_random)[0..47]`, then `key_block = PRF(master, "key expansion", server_random+client_random)` sliced into the **AES-128-GCM** client/server write keys (16 B each) and IVs (4 B each). Only **x25519** (named curve 0x001d) is wired up; if a server picks another curve it says so and stops. **Two-layer verification.** Offline, a new **`tlskeytest`** command runs a **key-schedule known-answer test** — pre-master = the RFC 7748 §6.1 X25519 shared secret, fixed randoms → a master secret and key block that an **independent PRF implementation** produced, so a wrong label or a swapped client/server-random order is caught here rather than only at a real server's Finished: **2/2 PASS**. Live, **`tls example.com` performed a real ECDHE exchange with the actual server** — ServerHello **0xc02b**, a 4-cert chain, **ServerKeyExchange curve 0x1d (x25519)** — and NyxOS **computed the 32-byte pre-master secret from the server's own ephemeral key**, then the **master secret** and the **AES-128-GCM key block** (client/server write keys shown), **0 faults**. **Known limits, stated in the output:** the ephemeral private key still uses the **non-crypto RNG** (fine to prove the mechanism, NOT for security — a CSPRNG is a later step), and the server's signature over its key is not verified yet (needs X.509). **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1501.1 -> 1504.6 KB (all in `tls.c`). **Next TLS steps:** **AES-128-GCM** record protection (encrypt/decrypt + GHASH) → send **ClientKeyExchange + ChangeCipherSpec + Finished** and verify the server's Finished → **decrypt application-data** → then `https` in `http_get`/Selene. |
| v5.9.50 | **TLS, step 3 — the TLS 1.2 PRF (P_SHA256), the key-derivation function, proven against RFC 4231 + the canonical PRF vector.** TLS turns the ECDHE **pre-master secret** into the **master secret**, and the master secret into the **AES keys and IVs**, by running the RFC 5246 §5 **PRF**; for the suite example.com chose (0xc02b) that PRF is **P_SHA256**: `P_SHA256(secret, seed) = HMAC(secret, A(1)+seed) || HMAC(secret, A(2)+seed) || …` with `A(0)=seed`, `A(i)=HMAC(secret, A(i-1))`, and `PRF(secret, label, seed) = P_SHA256(secret, label||seed)`. New **`tls_prf.c`** implements exactly this. **HMAC-SHA256 already existed** in the kernel (`sha256.c`, used by the password KDF), so the PRF is a thin, exact layer on top — no new hashing code. It is a **standalone primitive** this step: the handshake wiring (compute the pre-master secret via X25519, then call this to make the master secret + key block) is the next increment. Exposed as a **`prftest`** command. **Verified live in-guest** — `prftest` printed **3/3 vectors passed, 0 faults**: **HMAC-SHA256** against **RFC 4231** Test Cases 1 and 2 (confirming the existing HMAC is correct for this use), and the **TLS 1.2 P_SHA256** expansion against the canonical `secret + "test label" + seed → 100 bytes` test vector (the one every TLS library ships). **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1498.8 -> 1501.1 KB (`tls_prf.o`). **Next TLS steps:** wire the ECDHE agreement into the handshake (parse the server's ServerKeyExchange public key, generate our ephemeral X25519 keypair, `x25519()` → the pre-master secret, then this PRF → master secret + client/server AES keys and IVs) → **AES-128-GCM** → the **Finished** exchange → decrypt application-data records → `https` in `http_get`/Selene. |
| v5.9.49 | **TLS, step 2 — X25519, the first cryptographic primitive (Curve25519 ECDH), proven against the RFC 7748 vectors.** example.com negotiated **ECDHE** (0xc02b) and offered an X25519 key, so the ephemeral Diffie-Hellman function is the first piece of real cryptography on the road to reading an https page. New **`curve25519.c`** implements **X25519** exactly as RFC 7748 specifies — the **Montgomery ladder** over the prime field **GF(2^255-19)** — and is **constant-time**: no secret-dependent branches or memory indexing (the ladder swaps state with a bit-mask, not an `if`), so it leaks nothing through timing. The field arithmetic follows the well-known **TweetNaCl** reference (public domain): a field element is 16 limbs of ~16 bits held in signed 64-bit words, so limb products can never overflow, and only +, -, *, shifts and bitwise ops are used — **no division** — which suits a freestanding kernel with no libgcc soft-arithmetic. Exposed as an **`x25519test`** command that runs the known-answer vectors; `x25519_base()` derives a public key from a private scalar (scalar times basepoint u=9). This is a **standalone primitive** for now — nothing is wired into the handshake yet; that is the next increment (parse the server's ECDHE key, compute the shared secret, then the key schedule). **Verified live in-guest** — `x25519test` printed **5/5 vectors passed, 0 faults**: both RFC 7748 §5.2 scalar-mult KATs, Alice's and Bob's public keys (§6.1 base-point multiplications), and the **ECDH shared secret computed independently from each side — they agree** (`4a5d9d…161742`), the exact property the whole handshake rests on. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1493.5 -> 1498.8 KB (`curve25519.o`). **Next TLS steps:** wire X25519 into the handshake (our ephemeral keypair against the server's ECDHE key → the pre-master secret) + the **TLS-PRF key schedule** → **AES-128-GCM** → the **Finished** exchange → decrypt application-data records → then `https` in `http_get`/Selene. |
| v5.9.48 | **TLS, step 1 — NyxOS speaks TLS with a real https server (ClientHello → ServerHello).** The https arc begins. `tls.c` implements the **TLS record layer** and a real **TLS 1.2 ClientHello** — SNI, a cipher-suite list (ECDHE-RSA/ECDSA and RSA AES-GCM/CBC), `supported_groups` (x25519, secp256r1), `ec_point_formats`, `signature_algorithms` and `supported_versions` — sent over the existing TCP stack, then parses the server's whole flight: **ServerHello** (the negotiated cipher), **Certificate** (the chain), **ServerKeyExchange** (the ephemeral ECDHE key) and **ServerHelloDone**. There is **no cryptography yet** — the ClientHello random uses a non-crypto PRNG and nothing is decrypted; this step only proves the wire format and that a real server will talk to us. Exposed as a **`tls <host>`** diagnostic command (byte buffers are static, not on the stack). **Verified live in-guest:** `tls example.com` connected to **example.com:443**, sent the ClientHello, and the real server replied — **ServerHello negotiated 0xc02b** (`TLS_ECDHE_ECDSA_WITH_AES_128_GCM_SHA256`, one of the suites offered), a **4-certificate chain** (leaf 1003 bytes), **ServerKeyExchange present**, **ServerHelloDone**. That both confirms the handshake framing AND reveals the server wants **ECDHE** — pinning the next step to **X25519**. (The *first* lookup after boot hit an ARP-cold-start DNS miss — `dns_resolve` sends its query before the resolver's MAC is cached and doesn't retry once ARP resolves; the retry, ARP now warm, succeeded. Pre-existing DNS quirk, affects any first-lookup-after-boot, not TLS-specific.) **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1490.1 -> 1493.5 KB (`tls.o`). **Next TLS steps:** X25519 key exchange + the TLS-PRF key schedule → AES-128-GCM → the Finished exchange → decrypt application data → then wire `https` into `http_get`/Selene. |
| v5.9.47 | **Selene reads like a page, not a wall of text — headings, paragraphs, lists and rules.** The renderer stripped every tag to a flat run of collapsed-whitespace text, so a real page came out as one undifferentiated paragraph. This gives the strip **block-level layout**: a small `sel_ensure_nl(want, cap)` helper appends 1 or 2 trailing newlines (a line break vs. a blank line) while collapsing runs, so `<p>`/`<div>`/`<ul>`/`<table>`/`<section>`/… now produce a **paragraph break** (a blank line), `<br>`/`<tr>`/`<dd>`/`<dt>` a single line break, `<li>` a `"- "` **bullet** on its own line, and `<hr>` a **horizontal rule**. Headings `<h1>`–`<h6>` get a paragraph break, and `<h1>`/`<h2>` text is **upper-cased** for emphasis (the font is a single fixed weight, so caps is the cheap, ASCII-safe way to make a heading stand out — no per-line style machinery, no draw change). It's all contained in the HTML-strip pass: the wrap, the link grid, the draw path and the ctx are untouched, so it composes cleanly with the v5.9.45 links. **Verified live:** `example.com` now renders **`EXAMPLE DOMAIN`** as an emphasised heading with a blank line under it, the body paragraph separated, and the **`Learn more`** link in its own spaced paragraph — where before it was three lines jammed together. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1489.4 -> 1490.1 KB. **Why this before forms:** HTML forms are only useful against sites you can reach, and search engines/logins all force **https** — so forms are gated on TLS. This readability win pays off on every HTTP page you *can* load today; **TLS remains the real next unlock** (a big, dedicated arc). **Next:** TLS for `https`, then forms. |
| v5.9.46 | **Auto-DHCP at boot — the network is ready for everything, no manual `dhcp`.** Selene (and `httpget`, `ping`, …) needed an IP, which until now meant typing `dhcp` first; Selene worked around it by DHCPing on its own first load. This makes it a boot step instead: right after the network stack and TCP come up in `kernel_main`, if a real (non-loopback) NIC registered, the kernel leases an IP once via `dhcp_request()`. Because `dhcp_request` drives the net itself and returns as soon as the server ACKs (QEMU's SLIRP answers immediately), it adds only ~1-2 s to boot; and it is **guarded on a NIC being present**, so a diskless/netless boot with no `-nic` skips it entirely and never stalls on retries. The bootsplash shows "Requesting an IP via DHCP..." while it runs. **Verified both ways in-guest:** with `-nic user,model=rtl8139` the serial log shows the full lease at boot (`DISCOVER → OFFER 10.0.2.15 → REQUEST → ACK IP=10.0.2.15 gw=10.0.2.2`) *before* the login screen, and opening Selene then loads example.com with **no manual `dhcp`**; booting with **no NIC** shows zero DHCP traffic and reaches the login screen normally (the guard skipped it). Selene's own on-load DHCP fallback stays as a safety net. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1489.2 -> 1489.4 KB (a dozen lines in `kernel_main`). **Next Selene steps:** TLS for `https` (the big one), then form inputs. |
| v5.9.45 | **Selene grows up — clickable links, keyboard link-navigation, and a Back history.** The browser could fetch and render, but the rendered text was inert. Now `<a href>` links are captured during the HTML strip and rendered as **blue, underlined** text, and you can actually follow them. The mechanism is a **parallel per-character link-id grid** (`link_of`, a `uint8` per cell built alongside the word-wrapped `lines`), so any rendered character knows which link it belongs to — which is what makes both colouring and hit-testing possible. **Tab** cycles a selection through the links (wrapping back to the URL bar), the selected link gets a highlight box and the **status bar shows its target URL** (like a real browser's hover); **Enter** follows the selected link (or, with nothing selected, loads the URL bar); a **mouse click** follows the link under the cursor (`selene_win_click` maps the click to a link, the new **"<" Back button**, or the URL bar). Hrefs are resolved against the loaded page's base: **root-relative** (`/x`), **relative** (`x` against the base path's directory), and **protocol-relative** (`//host`) all work, `https://` is downgraded to `http://` (still no TLS), and `#fragment` / `mailto:` / `javascript:` are skipped. A per-window **history stack** powers Back — the "<" button, and **Backspace** when the URL bar is untouched (once you edit the URL, Backspace deletes a character instead). **Verified live in-guest, entirely by keyboard** (so it's headless-reachable, unlike a mouse click): `example.com` rendered "Learn more" blue+underlined with "1 links" in the status; **Tab** highlighted it and the status showed the resolved `http://iana.org/domains/example`; **Enter** followed it to a real **301** page (URL bar updated, the 301's own "here" link parsed, the Back button lit up); **Backspace** went **Back** to `example.com` (`200 OK`, Back button dimmed). **No new syscalls** (still 57; Selene rides `http_get`); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1485.3 -> 1489.2 KB. **Next Selene steps:** auto-DHCP at boot (so the net is ready without a manual `dhcp`), then TLS for `https` (the big one — needs a crypto layer), and eventually form inputs. |
| v5.9.44 | **Selene — the NyxOS web browser fetches and renders the real internet.** The user set a new arc — "a browser and internet connection" — and gave it a brand name (chosen via AskUserQuestion): **Selene**, the Greek moon goddess, on-theme with Nyx. Investigation first (per the standing lesson): the network stack was **already complete** — RTL8139 + DHCP + DNS + full TCP + an `http_get()` client, exposed as `httpget`. Verified end-to-end before building anything: booted `-nic user,model=rtl8139` (QEMU SLIRP NAT), `dhcp` pulled `10.0.2.15`, and `httpget example.com` returned a real **HTTP 200** with the live page. So this version is the GUI browser on top. **`selene_win.c`** is a window with a URL bar, a rendered page, and a status line.
