On open it ensures connectivity (runs DHCP if the iface has no IP), parses the URL (`http[s]://host[:port][/path]`, reusing the `httpget` shape), calls `http_get()` — which drives the network itself, so it just blocks briefly like a foreground app — then **strips the HTML to text**: `<script>`/`<style>` blocks dropped, a handful of entities decoded (`&amp; &lt; &gt; &quot; &nbsp;` + numeric), `<title>` pulled out for the status bar, block tags turned into line breaks, whitespace collapsed, and the result **word-wrapped** (hard-splitting over-long tokens) into scrollable lines. Chrome: a **crescent-moon** toolbar glyph, an editable URL bar with a caret, a light page area with a scrollbar, and a status line (`200 OK  -  <title>  (N bytes)`). Controls: type to edit the URL, **Enter** to load, **arrows / wheel / PgUp / PgDn / Home / End** to scroll, Backspace to edit. HTTP only — no TLS yet. It opens showing its default page (`example.com`) rather than blank. Launched by a **`selene` command** and a **10th desktop icon** (a crescent moon on a night sky — a new `emblem_selene`). **Also fixed `http.c`: it now decodes `Transfer-Encoding: chunked`** — example.com is chunked, and the raw chunk-size markers (`22f` … `0`) had been leaking into both `httpget` output and the first Selene render; de-chunking removed them, so the browser shows clean text. **Verified live in-guest:** `selene` opened the browser, which auto-DHCP'd, resolved DNS, TCP-connected and fetched **http://example.com**, rendering the clean "Example Domain … Avoid use in operations. Learn more" with **"200 OK - Example Domain"** in the status bar (a zoom confirmed the chunk markers are gone). **No new syscalls** (still 57; Selene rides the existing `http_get`); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1478.1 -> 1485.3 KB (`selene_win.o` + the de-chunk). **Next Selene steps:** clickable links (parse `<a href>`, map a click to a load) + Back/history, then more sites, and eventually TLS for `https`. Headless testing note: the browser is mouse-and-keyboard, and real URLs need punctuation the ES-layout `sendkey` mangles — run `layout us` first, or rely on the pre-filled default; see [[nyxos-gui-click-testing]]. |
| v5.9.43 | **Tetris — the fifth game, and the most mechanically complete one yet.** The games track continues (user: "ve con tetris"). `tetris_win.c` is a full Tetris on the same in-kernel + `on_tick` pattern as Pong/Snake: a 10x20 well beside a side panel showing the **NEXT** piece, **SCORE**, **LINES** and **LEVEL**. The seven tetrominoes and their four rotations are a compact **4x4-bitmask table** (`TET_SHAPES[7][4]`, cell `(r,c)` filled when `shape & (0x8000 >> (r*4+c))`), so rotation is just an index change and one `fits()` collision test against the walls, floor and locked cells. Gravity is the game-tick: the piece drops one row every `drop_ticks` frames, and `drop_ticks` shrinks as the level rises (level = 1 + lines/10). Controls: **Left/Right** move, **Up/W** rotates (with a small `{0,-1,1,-2,2}` wall kick so it still turns against a wall), **Down/S** soft-drops, **Space** hard-drops, **R** restarts. Locking a piece clears any full lines (classic **40/100/300/1200 × level** scoring), and a spawn that doesn't fit is game over. Each piece keeps its canonical colour (cyan I, blue J, orange L, yellow O, green S, purple T, red Z) with a 1px top bevel so the stack reads as blocks. It joins the shelf like the others: a **`tetris` command** and a fifth **Games-folder emblem** (a little well with coloured rubble and a falling T), so `GAMES_COUNT` 4 -> 5 and the folder auto-widens to a five-column 640 px row. **Verified live in-guest** (keyboard-driven, so fully reachable headless): `tetris` opened the window with a piece falling and the panel reading NEXT/SCORE 0/LINES 0/LEVEL 1; driving move + rotate + hard-drop (`Space`) a dozen times stacked pieces across the floor in their per-type colours (a zoom shows the mixed-colour rubble and a purple T mid-fall), confirming spawn, gravity, movement, rotation, hard-drop, locking and the next-piece hand-off. The five-emblem folder was confirmed with the same temporary auto-open used since v5.9.40, then removed; final `nyx-kernel.bin` is the clean 1478.1 KB. **No new syscalls** (still 57); 1472.2 -> 1478.1 KB (`tetris_win.o`). **Next — a new arc the user set:** basic-OS essentials, specifically a **web browser and real internet connectivity**, with the browser given **its own brand name** (not a generic "Browser"). NyxOS already has the TCP/DNS/HTTP plumbing (`tcp.c`, `dns.c`, `http.c`), so the arc is: reach the real network out of QEMU, a minimal HTML fetch+render, and a named GUI browser window. |
| v5.9.42 | **Snake — a second in-kernel game, and the Games folder grows to four.** With Pong's animation machinery already in place, a second classic was cheap and the user is enjoying the games, so this adds **Snake** (`snake_win.c`) reusing the exact `on_tick` game-tick from v5.9.39. The client area is a score strip over a 22x16 grid of 16 px cells; the tick steps the snake once every `STEP_TICKS` (5) frames so it moves at a playable ~6 cells/sec rather than the 30 fps repaint rate. **Arrows / WASD** steer, eating the food grows the snake and scores, and hitting a wall or itself ends the game with a **GAME OVER — press R** overlay (R restarts). Two details that make it play right: direction changes are **queued** (`ndx/ndy`) and only committed at the step, and a **180-degree reversal is rejected**, so a fast double-tap can't fold the head back into the neck for a false death; and the self-collision check **exempts the tail cell** (which is about to move away) *unless* the snake is about to grow. Food goes on a random free cell via a splitmix64 RNG seeded from `get_ticks()`. It joins the ecosystem the same way Pong did: a **`snake` shell command** and a fourth **Games-folder emblem** (a green coiled snake with a red pellet), so `GAMES_COUNT` goes 3 -> 4 and the folder auto-widens to a four-column 520 px row. **Verified live — and Snake is fully keyboard-driven, so unlike a mouse click this IS reachable headless:** `snake` opened the window; the snake auto-ran right into the wall and showed **GAME OVER**; **R** restarted it and **down** turned it vertical (a zoom shows three green cells with a brighter head, the red food, `Score: 0` and the grid — steering, animation, wall-death and restart all confirmed in-guest). The four-emblem folder was confirmed by the same temporary auto-open used in v5.9.40 (all four emblems + labels render, the wider layout fits), then removed; final `nyx-kernel.bin` is the clean 1472.2 KB. **No new syscalls** (still 57); 1468.7 -> 1472.2 KB (the new `snake_win.o`). **Next:** more classics (Tetris, Breakout, 2048) if the games track continues, otherwise desktop polish or the T1 kernel-internals backlog. |

| v5.9.40 | **A "Games" desktop folder gathers Minesweeper, DOOM and Pong — and Pong gets a clickable icon, like DOOM.** Pong shipped in v5.9.39 with only a `pong` command; the user asked for it to be launchable from an icon "just like DOOM", and for a desktop folder that visually lists the games. Both, in one move: the two standalone **Mines** and **DOOM** desktop icons are replaced by a single **Games** icon — drawn as a manila folder (tab + body) rather than the generic app square, because it is a container, not an app — and clicking it opens an in-kernel window (`games_win.c`) that lists the three games as clickable emblems. Each emblem is a little caricature of the game so the shelf reads at a glance: **Minesweeper** is a 3x3 tile grid with a mine and a red flag, **DOOM** is a white skull on hell-red, **Pong** is a dark court with cyan/red paddles, a dashed net and the ball. Clicking one launches it; Pong now has its icon on equal footing with DOOM (both live in the folder), and the desktop is tidier — one folder instead of three scattered game icons, which is what "the folder is *where* Mines, DOOM and Pong will be" asked for. Both games also keep their `pong`/`doom` shell commands, and Minesweeper stays in the Start menu. **The wiring keeps one definition per action:** `launch_minesweeper()` is extracted from `do_start_menu_action`'s `case 12` so the Start menu, the (old) icon and the folder all open Minesweeper through the same function; `launch_games_folder()` (desktop icon `action 14`) opens the folder; and **`games_win_click` hit-tests the emblem grid with the exact `icon_origin` geometry the draw path uses** — so a click on a visible emblem always maps to the right game — then dispatches to `launch_minesweeper` / `launch_doom_windowed` / `launch_pong`. The desktop icon table drops from 10 entries to 9. **Verified without a real click, because headless QEMU still can't deliver a PS/2 button** (the v5.9.36/rc17 limitation, re-confirmed here: a monitor-driven press on the Menu button did not open the Start menu — `mouse_move` positions the cursor but `mouse_button` emits no packet the guest acts on): (1) the desktop renders the **Games** manila-folder icon, and opening the folder renders all three emblems with their labels; (2) a **temporary synthetic `on_click` at the Pong emblem's centre — the real `games_win_click` path — launched Pong** (its window appeared with a "Pong" taskbar entry), proving the hit-test and dispatch end to end. Both temporary verification hooks were removed for the shipped build (final `nyx-kernel.bin` back to the clean 1466.8 KB). Minesweeper and DOOM take the identical `switch` calling launchers already proven — DOOM windowed (v5.9.37), Pong (v5.9.39), Minesweeper (existing). **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1464.5 -> 1466.8 KB (the new `games_win.o`). **Next:** the games shelf is complete; the natural follow-ups are a movable / background-composited DOOM window (the v5.9.37 known limit) or returning to the T1 kernel-internals track. |
| v5.9.39 | **Pong — an in-kernel GUI game, and the compositor's first ANIMATED window.** Windows were event-driven (redraw on click/key); nothing moved on its own. This adds a per-window `on_tick` callback and a ~30 fps game-tick in the compositor loop (`now - game_tick >= 33`, separate from the 1 Hz clock) that calls every visible window's `on_tick` and recomposites — so the Pong ball moves smoothly without any input. **`pong_win.c`** is the game: the court fills the client (`PONG_W x PONG_H`), the ball bounces off the top/bottom walls and both paddles (with "spin" added from where on the paddle it lands), the AI paddle chases the ball at a capped speed so a human can win, and a miss scores + re-centres. The **player's paddle tracks the mouse** (`on_mousemove`) — classic paddle control — with arrows / W-S as a fallback. Launched by the `pong` shell command (and, next, the Games folder). **Verified by screendump:** the window renders the court, centre net, both paddles, the ball and the score; three shots over a couple of seconds show the ball in different places and the score climbing (0 -> 17 -> 87 while the player paddle sat still and the AI scored); and injecting a mouse-move up through the monitor moved the cyan player paddle to the top of the court, confirming `on_mousemove` control. **No new syscalls** (still 57); `nyx-kernel.bin` 1462.0 -> 1464.5 KB (the new `pong_win.o`). **Next (v5.9.40): a "Games" desktop folder** that visually lists Minesweeper, DOOM and Pong and launches each. |
| v5.9.38 | **Title-bar buttons made pixel-consistent — the maximize icon was malformed on every window.** A user with an eye for it flagged a few-px chrome misalignment "in almost all windows"; a 10x crop of a title bar found it. `draw_max_button` drew the square's top and bottom edges out to `x+size-1` while the left/right verticals stopped at `x+size-4`, so the outline had two 3px horizontal stubs poking past the right side on every window's maximize button; the minimize dash was separately 2px wider than the close X and started a pixel further left. All three now share one inner box — `[x+P, x+E] x [y+P, y+E]`, `P=4` — so the dash, a clean square, and the X render as a uniform 8x8 set, centered the same way. **Verified** by re-cropping the same title bar at 10x: the maximize glyph is a clean closed square (was stubbed) and the three marks line up. Compositor-only, **no new syscalls** (still 57). |
| v5.9.37 | **DOOM runs in a WINDOW now, not fullscreen — you can see the desktop around it.** The user asked for windowed DOOM; this delivers it by rerouting the frame instead of taking the whole screen. `launch_doom_windowed()` opens a plain compositor window titled **"DOOM  -  Ctrl-C to quit"** (640x400 client = 2x the native 320x200), then foreground-runs `/doom.elf`. The one change that makes it windowed: **`SYS_FBPRESENT` is rerouted** — when a DOOM window is open, `doom_window_present()` copies the frame into a `doom_frame` buffer and returns "handled" instead of letting `fb_present_kbuf` blit it fullscreen. Because the fullscreen path never runs, `fb_fullscreen_active()` stays false, so the compositor keeps publishing the desktop normally; the DOOM window's draw callback nearest-neighbour-scales `doom_frame` into its client area, so the game composites in like any other window. It runs foreground (the proven `gui_launch_elf` path from v5.9.36), and `run_foreground_elf`'s `compositor_redraw_now()`+`sleep()` pump is what repaints the window each iteration. Both entry points use it: the `doom` shell command is now a builtin (`cmd_doom`) and the red desktop icon (`case 13`) both call `launch_doom_windowed`. On exit the window is torn down. **Verified by screendump:** typing `doom` opened a titled DOOM window rendering **E1M1 + the full HUD** with the Welcome/About/Terminal windows, the desktop icons, the moon and a "DOOM" taskbar entry all visible around it; **Ctrl-C** quit cleanly (`[exec] PID 8 exited (code 130)` — SIGINT via the foreground `g_foreground_pid`) and the window vanished, leaving a normal desktop with DOOM's console log in the terminal. **Known limits (v1):** the window is fixed-position — the compositor is blocked in the foreground pump while DOOM runs, so it can't be dragged or closed with the mouse (Ctrl-C, or ESC → Quit Game, exits); and the windowed framerate is the pump rate (~16 fps, vs fullscreen's native 35) — a smoother, movable window would need DOOM scheduled in the *background* with the compositor's own loop compositing it, which today is unreliable (the v5.9.35 background-spawn is exactly what failed). **No new syscalls** (still 57 — this reuses SYS_FBPRESENT, just routes its output). **Separately, the reported "app content misaligned" was investigated but not reproduced:** the app draw callbacks (Calc, Settings, …) correctly use the client origin `(cx, cy)` the compositor hands them, and the classic TITLE_H-offset bug the header warns about is already fixed in the hit-tests — so the specific misaligned app still needs to be identified (a screenshot). |
| v5.9.36 | **The DOOM desktop icon actually launches the game now — v5.9.35 wired the icon but clicking it did nothing.** Two real bugs, both reported by the user running it on the metal. **(1) The launcher never attached the disk.** `run.ps1` built its QEMU command with `-cdrom` but no `-hda ext2-test.img`, so NyxOS booted with no `/mnt` and DOOM — command *or* icon — could not find `/mnt/doom1.wad` and quit instantly. That is why DOOM "didn't work" at all. Fixed: `run.ps1` appends `-hda ext2-test.img` when the image exists and bumps RAM 256M→512M (a one-line encoding trap fixed in passing: an em-dash inside a `Write-Host` string made PowerShell 5.1, reading the file as its ANSI codepage, see a stray `"` and fail to parse — ASCII only now). Verified live on the user's machine: serial shows `[EXT2] Mounted /mnt` then DOOM's `I_FinishUpdate` render loop, 0 faults, driven through the HMP monitor from Windows python (computer-use can't attach to the QEMU window — a portable exe, not a registered app). **(2) The icon's action spawned DOOM but never scheduled it.** `do_start_menu_action`'s `case 13` used a bare `spawn_user_path("/doom.elf")`; from the compositor that creates the process but returns immediately, and the child never got the CPU — the click looked dead even after waiting. The shell's `doom` works because `execute_command` routes to `run_foreground_elf`, which after the spawn **pumps the desktop and blocks** — its `compositor_redraw_now(); sleep(60);` loop is what yields so the child runs and its `SYS_FBPRESENT` fullscreen takeover is serviced. Fix: that function is now exposed as **`gui_launch_elf()`** and `case 13` calls it, so the icon takes the exact proven path the command does (blocking the compositor there is the same call chain the terminal already uses). **Verified through the real icon action:** headless QEMU can position a cursor but cannot deliver a working PS/2 button press (the rc17 limitation), so a temporary `F9 → do_start_menu_action(13)` shim was added to fire action 13 from the keyboard — `sendkey f9` launched DOOM through the identical path (`I_InitGraphics: 320x200, bpp 32` + the `I_FinishUpdate` loop), `ESC` reached the DOOM title/menu, and `Ctrl-C` exited cleanly (`[exec] PID 8 exited (code 130)` — SIGINT via the foreground `g_foreground_pid`). The shim was removed for the shipped build. **Controls, for the record** (the user's other two notes were DOOM's normal behaviour, not bugs): DOOM boots into its attract-mode demo; **ESC** opens the menu (New Game → episode → skill for difficulty), and **Ctrl-C** (or ESC → Quit Game) exits back to the desktop. **No new syscalls** (still 57); the only kernel change is exposing the existing foreground-run. **Next (v5.9.37): windowed DOOM** — render into a GUI window instead of taking the whole framebuffer (the user asked), which also gives a title-bar close button and per-window input focus. |
| v5.9.35 | **DOOM gets a desktop icon — launch the game by clicking, not by knowing the `doom` command.** v5.9.34 made DOOM runnable; this makes it a first-class desktop app. The compositor's desktop-icon set grows from 9 to 10 with a distinctive **hell-red "DOOM" tile**, and it is the first icon whose action does something fundamentally different from the rest: every other icon opens an in-kernel GUI *window* (a `window_create` + draw callback), but **DOOM is a real ring-3 ELF**, so its action (`case 13` in `do_start_menu_action`) calls **`spawn_user_path("/doom.elf")`** — the exact kernel entry point the shell uses when you type `doom` — dropping the game into the preemptive scheduler as its own process. It then takes the framebuffer over `SYS_FBPRESENT`, the compositor yields (`fb_fullscreen_active`), and on exit the desktop returns via the same falling-edge repaint as any fullscreen app. Because `doomgeneric_nyxos.c`'s `main()` injects `-iwad /mnt/doom1.wad` whenever it is launched with no arguments, the icon path — which spawns with `argc==0` — finds the shareware WAD on the ext2 disk exactly as the command path does; no separate wiring. The `doom` command stays (the icon is additive), and the red is a one-line special-case in `draw_icon_at` keyed on the icon's name so it survives drag-reordering (which swaps names and actions together). **Verified by screendump:** after login the desktop shows the ten icons with the red **DOOM** tile in the top row, and launching `/doom.elf` under this build still reaches `I_InitGraphics: 320x200, bpp 32` and the `[I_FinishUpdate]` render loop — so the icon's spawn target runs, and the compositor change caused no regression (the desktop, windows, and other icons render normally). The one link not exercised by an automated test is the physical mouse *press* on the tile: headless QEMU can position the guest cursor but cannot deliver a working PS/2 button packet through the monitor (the limitation the v5.9.0-rc17 GUI-verify work documented and worked around with a real host press), so the click→launch was verified *by construction* — action 13 is dispatched by the identical `do_start_menu_action(desktop_icon_actions[i])` path all nine working icons use, wired to the already-proven `spawn_user_path`. **No new syscalls** (still 57); **no kernel-internals change** beyond the compositor (`nyx-kernel.bin` 1460.6 → 1460.7 KB). **Next (v5.9.36): FPU/SSE context switching (`fxsave`/`fxrstor`)** — the recommended internal — so DOOM's XMM state is per-task safe now that it runs as a desktop app alongside the compositor and every other task, removing the "DOOM is the only SSE user" assumption v5.9.34 leaned on. |
| v5.9.34 | **DOOM RUNS — the original 1993 DOOM (shareware), playable on NyxOS. The "but can it run DOOM?" milestone, reached.** v5.9.33 produced a linking `doom.elf` that had never executed; this wires it in, feeds it the WAD, and drives it to the title screen and into a level. **Wiring:** a makefile rule builds `doom.elf` via `build_doom.sh` and adds it to `USER_ELFS`; `mkinitramfs` globs `user/*.elf`, so it ships in the initramfs as `/doom.elf` and the shell runs it as `doom` (kernel `1011.3 -> 1460.6 KB`, all of it the embedded 448 KB ELF). The shareware **doom1.wad** (md5 `f0cefca4…`, IWAD, ~4 MB — freely redistributable, user-supplied) was written into `ext2-test.img` with `debugfs`, and `main()` injects `-iwad /mnt/doom1.wad` so a bare `doom` finds it. **Then three bugs stood between "links" and "runs", each caught by serial instrumentation and fixed at the root.** (1) **The WAD read returned nothing.** `fopen` malloc'd a 4 MB buffer and `SYS_READ`'d the file into it, but the read reported 0 bytes even though the header was right — because `copy_to_user` walks the *user* page tables and **cannot fault a not-yet-present lazy `sbrk` page in** (the exact hazard the getdents note in `syscall.c` documents), so the copy into an untouched buffer fails. Fix: `fopen` `memset`s the buffer first — the same page-touching `ls` already does — and the whole 4 MB lands (`got=4196020`, header `IWAD`). (2) **A NULL function-pointer call at RIP 0 in `I_Init`.** `doom_userlibc.c` had stubbed `DG_sound_module`/`DG_music_module` as `void* = 0`, but the engine dereferences them as full `sound_module_t`/`music_module_t` structs (their real definitions live in the excluded SDL/Allegro backends), so `InitSfxModule` read a garbage `Init` pointer off an 8-byte stub and called it. Fix: a new `user/doom_nullsound.c` supplies real no-op structs with `num_sound_devices = 0` (so `InitSfxModule` finds no device and skips sound cleanly) and every function pointer a valid no-op. (3) **`#UD` (invalid opcode) at the first `movups %xmm0`.** DOOM's stock x86_64 codegen uses XMM registers for float math *and* for 16-byte struct copies (112 SSE instructions in the ELF), but the boot path only enables PAE/long-mode/paging — `CR4.OSFXSR` stays clear, so any SSE instruction faults. Fix: `kernel_main` now calls `enable_sse_fpu()` (clear `CR0.EM`, set `CR0.MP`, set `CR4.OSFXSR`|`OSXMMEXCPT`, `fninit`). NyxOS's kernel and coreutils are all built `-mno-sse`, so DOOM is the *only* code that touches XMM — its vector state survives context switches for free, no per-task `fxsave` needed (a note flags that a second SSE program would change that). **Verified by screendump, the whole stack exercised at once:** `doom` boots to id's engine (`zone memory: 0x600000` — the 6 MB zone `sbrk`'d without trouble; `W_AddFile` accepts `/mnt/doom1.wad`; `I_InitGraphics: 320x200, bpp 32, RGBA 8888` — byte-identical to what `fbpresent` wants; `[I_FinishUpdate]` looping), renders **E1M1 with the full status bar** (AMMO 50 / HEALTH 100% / ARMS / the marine's face / ARMOR / BULL·SHEL·ROKT·CELL) scaled 320x200 -> 1024x768 by `SYS_FBPRESENT`, and **ESC brings up the DOOM main menu** (New Game / Options / Load Game / Save Game / Read This! / Quit Game, skull cursor) — proving `SYS_GETKEYEVENT` -> `DG_GetKey` input reaches the game. Video (v5.9.29), input (v5.9.30), timing, big-file VFS (the WAD off ext2), and enough heap are all working together under a real, demanding program. **One kernel feature** (SSE/FPU enablement); **no new syscalls** (still 57 — the DG_* hooks map onto fbpresent/getkeyevent/gettimeofday/nanosleep from v5.9.29-30). The milestone the roadmap set for after v5.9.40 is standing at v5.9.34. |
| v5.9.33 | **DOOM links — the whole engine now produces a userspace ELF (448 KB, zero undefined symbols).** v5.9.32 got 83/83 translation units compiling and pinned the link gap to 35 symbols; this closes it. Rather than reimplement a libc, the recovered `doom_utils.c` — the freestanding libc the old kernel port already used, which knows exactly what the engine needs — was copied to `user/doom_userlibc.c` and its handful of KERNEL touchpoints retargeted at NyxOS userspace syscalls: `malloc` becomes an `sbrk`-bump allocator (SYS_SBRK; the engine's own realloc copies `size` bytes so no block header is needed), `fopen` opens the path (and `/mnt/<name>`) and reads the whole file into a malloc'd buffer over `open`/`lseek`/`read`, `fclose`/`exit`/`serial_puts` go to `close`/`exit`/`write`, `mkdir` is a no-op, and `memcpy`/`memset` become `rep movsb`/`rep stosb` so they can't recurse into themselves under `-Os`. It also defines the globals the engine references — `doom_wad_data`/`doom_wad_size` left null so the WAD loads by file path, plus `stdin`/`stdout`/`stderr`, `errno`, and stubbed sound-module handles — and the seven small pieces the old code assumed the platform provided (`printf`/`vprintf`/`snprintf`/`putchar`/`puts`/`strstr`/`environ`). **`user/doomgeneric_nyxos.c`** stopped defining `DG_ScreenBuffer` (the engine owns it) and points it at its buffer in `DG_Init`, resolving the last conflict. **`user/build_doom.sh`** is the working recipe: every engine TU + the platform + `doom_userlibc.c` compiled with `-fcommon` (the pre-gcc10 tentative-definition merging the old code relies on) and `-fno-builtin -fno-tree-loop-distribute-patterns`, linked with `crt0.o` and NO libc.so — `doom_userlibc.c` IS the libc. **Result: compiles with 0 failures, links with 0 undefined, `user/doom.elf` = 448 KB.** Still not wired into the kernel build and not yet run — the ELF exists but has never executed. **Kernel untouched** (`nyx-kernel.bin` still 1011.3 KB). **Next (v5.9.34): wire `doom.elf` into the build/initramfs, get doom1.wad onto /mnt, and run it — the first execution, and the moment the ScreenBuffer→fbpresent and getkeyevent→doomkey paths get exercised for real.** **No new syscalls** (still 57). |
| v5.9.32 | **DOOM userspace build, step one: the whole engine compiles, and the link gap is pinned to 35 symbols.** Building on v5.9.31's recovery, this writes the NyxOS userspace platform layer and a build recipe, and gets a concrete measurement of how far off a runnable DOOM is. **`user/doomgeneric_nyxos.c`** is the ~90-line platform bridge — the six hooks doomgeneric asks every port to implement, wired to the syscalls: `DG_DrawFrame` issues `SYS_FBPRESENT` on the engine's 32bpp `pixel_t` buffer, `DG_GetKey` reads `SYS_GETKEYEVENT` and maps NyxOS raw scancodes to doomkeys (arrows/ctrl/space/enter/esc + a US positional letter+digit table for menus), `DG_GetTicksMs` and `DG_SleepMs` use `SYS_GETTIMEOFDAY` / `SYS_NANOSLEEP`. It talks to the kernel through a tiny inline `syscall` rather than including `user/syscall.h`, so DOOM's own freestanding libc shims are the only headers in play — no type clashes — and it compiles clean in isolation. **`user/build_doom.sh`** compiles every engine translation unit against `kernel/doom_src` (which ships its own `stdio.h`/`stdlib.h`/`string.h`/`math.h` shim headers) with the SDL/allegro/GUS sound backends and the other platforms' `doomgeneric_*.c` excluded, plus a non-CMAP256 (32bpp) define so the ScreenBuffer maps straight to fbpresent. **Result: 83 of 83 translation units compile, 0 failures** — the recovered shims are complete enough that id's engine builds unmodified. A trial link against `user/libc.so` (which already provides malloc/free/mem*/str*/printf) leaves **exactly 35 undefined symbols**, and they are all understood: the `stdio` FILE* family (fopen/fread/fseek/fwrite/ftell/fclose/fprintf/vfprintf/fflush/sscanf + stderr/stdout), a few stdlib/string/math bits (atof, calloc, realloc, strcasecmp, strncasecmp, strrchr, strdup, fabs, remove, rename, mkdir, exit, system, errno, vsnprintf), and a handful of DOOM/NyxOS globals and sound stubs (doom_wad_data/size, DG_sound_module/DG_music_module, serial_puts, libsamplerate). **The kernel is untouched** (`nyx-kernel.bin` still 1011.3 KB) — this is userspace scaffolding + a measurement, so nothing new runs yet; `doom_src` is not in the makefile's OBJS and the DOOM ELF isn't wired into the build until it links. **Next (v5.9.33): a userspace `doom_userlibc.c` that supplies those 35 symbols** — the stdio family over the `open`/`read`/`lseek` syscalls, the small stdlib/string/math gaps, and no-op sound stubs — then a full link, then doom1.wad on /mnt, then a screendump of the title screen. **No new syscalls** (still 57 — the engine's needs are met by fbpresent/getkeyevent/gettimeofday/nanosleep). |
| v5.9.31 | **The DOOM port begins — the doomgeneric engine recovered from git history, and the platform hooks scoped against the new syscalls.** This is a foundation increment, not a running feature: it recovers the source and confirms the plan; nothing new builds or runs yet. **The find:** the whole prior DOOM (the standard `doomgeneric` — DOOM's engine plus doomgeneric's portable platform abstraction) was still in git history. The v2.0.1 attempt integrated it INTO the kernel over VGA mode 13h, then commit `7372f0b` "Remove all DOOM code" deleted the lot (`kernel/doom_src/`, `doom_nyxos.c`, sound, VGA driver). So there is no re-vendoring to do — `git checkout 7372f0b~1 -- kernel/doom_src` restores 210 files (2.3 MB) intact. **Why the last two increments were the right groundwork:** `doomgeneric.h` declares exactly six platform hooks, and each maps cleanly onto what NyxOS now has — `DG_DrawFrame` -> `SYS_FBPRESENT` (v5.9.29; and with the non-CMAP256 build the engine's `pixel_t` ScreenBuffer is 32bpp `0x00RRGGBB`, byte-identical to what fbpresent already expects, so it is a direct handoff), `DG_GetKey` -> `SYS_GETKEYEVENT` (v5.9.30, mapping raw scancodes to doomkeys — and a game wanting physical keys is exactly why that path is raw), `DG_GetTicksMs` -> `gettimeofday`, `DG_SleepMs` -> `usleep`. The two hard hooks were built first on purpose. **The recovery does not touch the kernel** — `doom_src` is not in the makefile's OBJS, so `nyx-kernel.bin` is byte-identical to v5.9.30 (1011.3 KB); this commit is source + docs only. **What is NOT done, and is the real work ahead:** the old port ran in the kernel; the new one is a userspace ELF driven by those syscalls, which means getting ~30k lines of DOOM to compile FREESTANDING against `user/libc` — DOOM leans on `<stdio.h>` (FILE*/fopen/fread/fseek, 54 files), `<stdlib.h>` (38), `<string.h>` (30), `<ctype.h>`, `<math.h>`, `<fcntl.h>`. That is a multi-increment shim grind (compile -> fill a libc gap -> repeat), then a ~40-line userspace platform file, then getting **doom1.wad** (the freely-distributable shareware data, ~4 MB — a user-supplied asset, not code) onto `/mnt`, and finally a screendump of the title screen. **No new syscalls** (still 57 — the two the engine needs already exist). |
| v5.9.30 | **Raw key-event path — press AND release for a fullscreen game. Second DOOM-milestone enabler (DG_GetKey), after v5.9.29's video.** Every input path userspace had was cooked and press-only: the stdin line discipline hands over characters, and the keycode ring carries arrows/F-keys on the press edge. A game needs the opposite — it must know when a key is RELEASED (to stop moving, stop firing), and it wants PHYSICAL keys (WASD/arrows by position), not layout-translated characters. Neither ring delivers that. This adds a third ring in `keyboard.c`, the raw key-EVENT ring: the IRQ1 handler records EVERY make and break, independent of the cooked decode, as a layout-independent Set-1 scancode (bits 0-6 the code, bit 7 flagging an E0-extended arrow/nav key) plus the press/release bit — packed `(pressed << 8) | code`. It is filled and drained under the same `kbd_lock` as the other two rings (v5.9.23), and the E0 prefix is tracked on its own so an extended key like the up arrow (`E0 48`) becomes a stable code `0xC8` on both its make and its break. **`SYS_GETKEYEVENT` (56)** pops the next event, or returns -1 when the ring is empty — non-blocking, so a game polls it each frame; the userspace `getkeyevent(&pressed, &code)` wrapper unpacks it. Because the codes are physical scancodes, none of the ES/US layout logic is involved — the same reason a game wants them. **Verified by driving real keys through the monitor:** `keyevtest.elf` drained the events buffered while its own command line was typed, then polled while the harness sent `a`, space and the up arrow. It reported exactly six events — each key as a press THEN a release: `pressed=1 code=0x1e` / `pressed=0 code=0x1e` (a), `0x39` (space), and `0xc8` (up arrow, the E0 bit set) — all correct scancodes, every count 1/1, 0 faults. Key *release*, which the stdin path fundamentally cannot express, now reaches userspace. **One new syscall** (56 -> 57). With video (v5.9.29) and now input in place, the DOOM platform layer's two hard hooks — DG_DrawFrame and DG_GetKey — are both backed; DG_GetTicksMs/DG_SleepMs map to the existing time syscalls and the WAD loads over the VFS. Next: the doomgeneric port itself. |
| v5.9.29 | **Fullscreen framebuffer syscalls — a userspace program can finally put pixels on the screen. First step toward the v5.9.40 DOOM milestone.** Until now there was NO way for a ring-3 program to draw: the framebuffer belonged entirely to the in-kernel compositor, and userspace had zero video syscalls. That is the one missing half of a DOOM platform layer — timing (`get_ticks`/`sleep`), memory (`malloc`), and WAD-file I/O (the VFS) already exist; only "present a frame" did not. Two new syscalls fill it. **`SYS_FBINFO` (54)** hands back the screen geometry (width/height/bpp) so an app can size its render buffer. **`SYS_FBPRESENT` (55)** takes a `w x h` 32bpp buffer and blits it to the hardware framebuffer, **nearest-neighbour scaled to the full screen** — so DOOM's native 320x200 becomes a full 1024x768 without the app caring. The user buffer is copied into a reusable, grow-on-demand kernel scratch (validated, bounded to 8 MB) before the blit, so the blit routine `fb_present_kbuf` never dereferences a user VA. **The compositor yields cleanly.** A program that keeps calling `SYS_FBPRESENT` takes fullscreen ownership: `fb_present()` (the desktop's publish) becomes a no-op while `fb_fullscreen_active()` is true, so the desktop can't overwrite the app's frames. Ownership is a **self-releasing 500 ms timeout on the last present** — no reap hook, no explicit release; when the app stops presenting (exits), it lapses, and a falling-edge check in the compositor loop forces one recomposite so the desktop returns instead of leaving the app's last frame frozen. **Verified end to end with a screendump, the first pixels-from-userspace proof in the project.** `fbtest.elf` read the screen (`1024x768, 32 bpp`), rendered three vertical bars (R|G|B) into a 320x200 buffer, and presented 100 frames; a monitor `screendump` mid-run, sampled programmatically at 1/6, 1/2 and 5/6 width, returned **left=(255,0,0), mid=(0,255,0), right=(0,0,255)** — three saturated primaries filling the whole screen, so the scale-up, the BGRX colour mapping, and the compositor yield are all correct. 0 faults; `fbtest` exits 0 and the desktop comes back. **Two new syscalls** (54 -> 56). Next DOOM-prep: a fullscreen key-event path (`DG_GetKey`), then the doomgeneric port itself. |
| v5.9.28 | **Partial mprotect now splits the VMA too — the companion to v5.9.27, closing the mmap/mprotect precision pair.** `do_mprotect` rewrote the page-table flags of just the range (`vm_protect_range` is precise), but then set the `prot` field of the WHOLE overlapping VMA — so a page elsewhere in that VMA which had not faulted in yet would later materialise with the mprotected protection instead of its own. Concretely: mprotect the MIDDLE of an RW mapping to read-only, and the still-RW pages on either side silently became read-only the moment they were first touched, because the fault path reads `v->prot` and the whole VMA now said RO. **The fix reuses v5.9.27's split machinery, but mprotect KEEPS every piece** (the affected sub-range takes the new prot; the remainder keeps the old), so where munmap dropped/trimmed, mprotect carves: a back or front cut makes one new VMA, a middle band makes two (head old / band new / tail old). A new `vma_add_anon` helper fills the slots; a feasibility pass rejects up front (a file-backed VMA can't be split — the `file_buf[page - v->start]` snapshot would misalign — and a middle carve needs two free slots). **The one subtlety munmap didn't have:** the new-prot pieces overlap `[addr, end)` (munmap's freed tail started exactly at `end`, so it never did), so the apply loop first SNAPSHOTS the original overlapping VMAs and iterates that, or it would reprocess the pieces it just created. **Verified with a purpose-built `mprotecttest.elf`:** map four RW pages (none faulted in), mprotect the middle two to read-only, then write the two OUTSIDE pages — both succeed, so the split kept the flanks writable (all three checks pass and the "OUTSIDE-OK" milestone prints; the old build would have faulted on the first outside write). Then it writes into the read-only band, which faults exactly as it should: `[fault] pid 8 (mprotecttest): Page Fault (#14) ... fault-addr 0x100001000 ... err 0x7 ... cs=0x1b -> killed (signal 11)`. The address is `MMAP_BASE + 1 page` (the band), and `err 0x7` is present + **write** + user — a write to a *present read-only* page, i.e. the band's protection held. (v5.9.27's freed-hole probe faulted with `err 0x4`, not-present; each fix's fault code is its signature.) The kernel does not panic (`PANIC: 0`, shell keeps running). **No new syscalls** (still 54 — `mmap.c` + a userspace test). With this, both `munmap` and `mprotect` treat a partial range precisely at the VMA level, not just in the page tables. |
| v5.9.27 | **Partial munmap now splits the VMA — a real "the unmap silently didn't take" bug in the memory manager.** `do_munmap` freed the pages of any range, but only dropped a VMA that the range *fully* covered; a PARTIAL unmap (a hole in the middle, or trimming one end) left the VMA record spanning the whole original mapping. The pages were gone, but the stale VMA still matched — so the very next touch of the "unmapped" hole found the VMA in `vma_find` and `vm_handle_fault` handed back a brand-new zeroed page. munmap of part of a region did nothing durable: the memory came right back (zeroed), and a program that unmapped a sub-range and relied on that address faulting instead read zeroes. **The fix classifies each overlapping VMA by which ends fall inside the unmap range — a clean 2x2: full cover (drop), back-trim (`v->end = addr`), front-trim (`v->start = end`), and a middle hole (shrink to the head `[start, addr)` and add a fresh VMA for the tail `[end, old_end)`).** The one subtlety is file-backed maps: a faulted page copies from `file_buf[page - v->start]`, so moving `v->start` (front-trim or middle-split) would misalign that shared kmalloc'd snapshot — those two cases are refused for file-backed VMAs, while full-cover and back-trim (which leave `v->start` put) are fine. A feasibility pass runs BEFORE anything is freed: it rejects the unsupported file-backed cases and confirms a free VMA slot exists for a middle-split's new tail, so a refused munmap changes nothing. **Verified with a purpose-built `munmaptest.elf`.** It maps five demand-zero pages as one VMA, marks each, then punches out the middle page: the four surrounding pages keep their markers (the split touched neither the head nor the tail), and a following back-trim and front-trim likewise preserve their survivors — all six checks pass. Then the decisive probe reads the freed hole. On this build it faults exactly as it should: `[fault] pid 8 (munmaptest): Page Fault (#14) ... fault-addr 0x100002000 ... err 0x4 ... cs=0x1b -> killed (signal 11)` — the address is `MMAP_BASE + 2 pages` (the hole), `err 0x4` is a ring-3 **not-present** access (genuinely unmapped, not a refaulted zero page), and the process is killed with SIGSEGV while **the kernel does not panic** (`PANIC: 0`; the shell keeps running, `exit code 139`). The old build would instead have read 0 and printed the BUG line. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — `mmap.c` + a userspace test). Related, deferred: `do_mprotect` still applies a partial range's new protection to the whole overlapping VMA (only the page tables are precise) — the same split machinery would tighten it. |
| v5.9.26 | **EXT2 sector cache — the driver's first cache, write-through so it cannot corrupt the disk.** Until now every ext2 disk access went straight to the platter, and `ext2_read_block` fetched a block A SECTOR AT A TIME (`read_sectors(lba+i, 1, ...)` in a loop — one PIO command per 512 bytes). Worse, ext2 re-reads the same metadata constantly: the superblock, the group descriptors, the inode-table blocks and the block/inode bitmaps are read again on *every* path resolution and allocation. **The insight that makes a cache safe here: `read_sectors` / `write_sectors` are the sole disk choke points** — all block I/O and even the fixed 1 KB superblock funnel through them — so a cache placed there is coherent BY CONSTRUCTION: a write refreshes the cached line in place, a read consults it first, and there is no higher-level invalidation path to get wrong (the class of bug that makes filesystem caches dangerous, and the one the driver's own buffer comment warns "survives a reboot"). It is **write-through**, direct-mapped by sector number, 256 lines x 512 B = 128 KB of static BSS (zero-init, so it does not grow the kernel binary — 966.7 -> 967.7 KB is just the code). Write-through means the disk is always current: nothing to flush, and a crash can never lose a cached write. It **composes with the v5.9.25 `ext2_lock` for free** — `read_sectors`/`write_sectors` run only under that lock (except boot-time mount, which is single-core), so the cache lines need no lock of their own. Hit/miss counters are exposed through `df`. **Verified on three axes.** *Correctness:* under `smpbalance on`, five `write /mnt/fN mark<N>` -> `cat /mnt/fN` round-trips all read back correctly and `cat /mnt/base.txt` returned the right bytes 11 times — write-through kept every line coherent. *The definitive proof:* `e2fsck -fn` on the image after the run reported **exit 0, clean** (13/4096 files, 536/16384 blocks, all five passes) — a coherency bug would have surfaced as bad bitmaps or orphaned inodes. *Effectiveness:* `df` reported **384 hits / 200 misses (65% hit rate)** — 384 disk reads eliminated on an ordinary mixed workload. 0 faults. **No new syscalls** (still 54). (Timekeeping was scoped first but turned out already complete — `get_ticks()` is ms-since-boot at the 1000 Hz PIT, with `SYS_SLEEP`/`TIME`/`GETTIMEOFDAY`/`NANOSLEEP`/`ALARM` all present — so this took its place.) It is also the first step toward the v5.9.40 DOOM milestone: loading a multi-megabyte WAD off `/mnt` leans directly on this path. |
| v5.9.25 | **The EXT2 driver locked — the T1 follow-up beyond the planned trio, and the last major non-reentrant subsystem a user process can reach under `smpbalance`.** The driver keeps FIVE global scratch buffers (`ext2_bufs[5]` — block / bitmap / aux / aux2 / metadata), split by role precisely so a SAME-THREAD nested block read is safe (the code's own comment recounts how a single shared buffer once made `free_inode_blocks` walk the block bitmap as if it were an indirect block, "releasing arbitrary blocks across the disk" — corruption that survives a reboot). But those buffers are global, so two *cores* in the driver at once trample all five. The guard was `preempt_disable()` in the vfs.c `fs_*` mount wrappers — a coarse single-core "FS lock", by its own admission, and meaningless to another core. Two design facts shaped the fix. First, **the wrappers are not the only entry**: `auth.c` reads and writes the login passwd file by calling `ext2_resolve`/`ext2_read_file`/`ext2_mkdir`/`ext2_write_file` *directly*, so locking only the wrappers would leave a login check racing a `/mnt` op. Second, **ext2's public functions call each other** (`ext2_write_file`/`mkdir`/`unlink` → `ext2_resolve`), so a lock taken *inside* each public entry would self-deadlock (the spinlock isn't reentrant). Both point to the same model used for `net_lock` and `kbd_lock`: a **caller-held lock**. `ext2.c` exposes a tiny `ext2_lock_acquire()` / `ext2_lock_release(flags)` API over a private `spinlock_t`; the vfs.c wrappers and auth.c's passwd helpers wrap their `ext2_*` calls in it, while ext2.c's internal cross-calls run under the caller's hold and never re-acquire. `spin_lock_irqsave` again does both jobs — cross-core exclusion plus the `cli` that reproduces `preempt_disable`'s no-task-switch-mid-op property — and it is safe to hold across the whole operation including PIO block I/O because the ATA path is **polled** (no ATA IRQ handler in the `irq_install_handler` table), so nothing enters the driver from interrupt context. `ext2_mount` is intentionally left unlocked: it runs once at boot on the BSP before any AP or user process. Nesting is `ext2_lock -> kmalloc_lock` only (`get_buf_n` lazily allocates the buffers). **Verified on a 4-CPU boot with the ext2 disk attached** (`-hda ext2-test.img` — without it `ata_init` fails, `/mnt` never mounts, and the driver is never entered, so a disk-less run tests nothing; a real correction to how these are exercised). Under `smpbalance on`: the login itself drives the auth passwd path through the lock; then five rounds each did a foreground `write /mnt/fN mark<N>` → `cat /mnt/fN` round-trip **while a spawned background `cat /mnt/base.txt` read the disk on another core** — 5/5 foreground contents correct, 5/5 background reads correct, `ls /mnt` lists all files, **0 faults / 0 panics**. That is genuine concurrent cross-core ext2 entry with perfect integrity. As with `net_lock`, the justification is mechanistic — the shared buffers cannot be serialized across cores by a per-core counter, and the buffer-trample race is load-dependent so a quiet-host A/B would be the P0.1 trap — with the concurrent-load run as corroboration that the lock is correct and deadlock-free. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — internal locking). The `proc_sync` `preempt_disable` (the /proc tree + process table, not the ext2 driver) is a separate concern and stays. |
| v5.9.24 | **Network stack serialized under one `net_lock` — the capstone of the T1 arc (after the VFS node pool, mount table, and keyboard input).** The TCP/UDP/IP stack is entirely NON-reentrant, and it plus the socket table (`nsocks[32]`) and the per-UDP-socket datagram rings are all shared. The old guard was `preempt_disable()` in `kernel_poll_net` and the `nsock_*` TCP wrappers, with a discipline the code documents at length: the blocking socket calls (connect/accept/recv/recvfrom busy-poll in place, driving both endpoints) *release* preemption during their idle delay so a peer — e.g. the built-in loopback echo server on port 7 — can run and reply. Correct on one core, and worth preserving. But `preempt_disable()` is a per-core counter another core never reads (spinlock.h spells this out), so under `smpbalance` two processes on two cores enter the non-reentrant stack at the same instant — one draining the loopback ring while another mutates the same TCP conn, both claiming the same socket-table slot, both advancing the same UDP `dq` ring. `net_lock` (`spin_lock_irqsave`) is the subsystem's single giant lock: every atomic stack operation and every socket-table / ring mutation takes it, and — crucially — the blocking loops *release* it during their idle wait, exactly where they released preemption, so the client-holds-lock / server-needs-lock deadlock cannot form. `spin_lock_irqsave` does both jobs preempt_disable and cross-core exclusion need at once: the spin excludes other cores, and its `cli` preserves the same "no task switch mid-operation on this core" property (the timer can't fire to switch). The conversion covered every `preempt_disable` site and *added* locking to the shared state that had none — the free-slot claim in `nsock_create`/`nsock_accept` (two cores would otherwise seize one slot), the `dq` dequeue in `nsock_recvfrom` (vs. the enqueue in `nsock_udp_deliver`, which already runs locked from the RX poll), the auto-bind + send in `nsock_sendto`, `nsock_readable`, `nsock_bind`, and the whole `nsock_close` teardown (so a poll can't read a freed `dq`). It transitively covers `ip.c`'s loopback ring, since every `ip_send` / `ip_loopback_poll` reaches it only through a `net_lock` holder. **The NIC is poll-driven** (`eth_poll` under `kernel_poll_net`; the `irq_install_handler` table has no net entry), so nothing takes `net_lock` from interrupt context — the `irqsave` is purely for that same-core no-switch property. Nesting is `net_lock -> kmalloc_lock` only. **Verified** on a 4-CPU boot with `netstorm` — 4 forked kids, each its own TCP connection to 127.0.0.1:7, 25 checked echo rounds apiece (100 concurrent round-trips): **ALL CLEAN (0/4 corrupted) both without and with `smpbalance on`, 0 faults, exit(0)**. That the kids all connect and complete is the deadlock proof — the release-during-idle discipline lets the echo server acquire the lock between a client's polls. **On the honesty of the evidence:** an A/B against the old `preempt_disable` build ALSO ran `netstorm` clean under `smpbalance` (3 quiet-host runs, 0 faults) — which proves nothing, because this is a load-dependent cross-core race and a quiet run is exactly the P0.1 trap. So the justification is *mechanistic* (preempt_disable structurally cannot serialize across cores; net_lock does), with the netstorm passes as corroboration that the lock is correct and deadlock-free — not a reproduced-failure A/B. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — internal locking). This completes the planned T1 trio (VFS / terminal / net). The remaining `preempt_disable` sites are a different layer — the `ext2` driver wrappers in `vfs.c` (a non-reentrant on-disk FS) and process teardown — candidates for a follow-up increment. |
| v5.9.23 | **Keyboard input path locked — third T1 increment, the terminal's line discipline made SMP-safe.** After the VFS node pool (v5.9.21) and mount table (v5.9.22), the next unlocked shared structure sits under every keystroke: the keyboard driver's two ring buffers — `kbd_buffer` (ASCII, feeds stdin) and `kbd_keycodes` (extended keys, feeds window management) — plus the modifier state machine (shift/ctrl/alt/caps/altgr/e0) inside `scancode_to_ascii`. **The producer is the IRQ1 handler; the consumers (`getchar_poll`, `getkey_poll`) can run on any core** once `smpbalance` spreads a reader off the BSP — with a subtle third wrinkle: `getchar_poll`'s hardware-poll fallback re-enters `scancode_to_ascii` from a *second* context, so the keycode ring and modifier globals had two possible writers, not one. Two cores in `getchar_poll` could both see `tail != head`, both read `kbd_buffer[tail]`, and both advance `tail` — one keystroke delivered twice and an index corrupted. The old code leaned on a single-core invariant, spelled out in `syscall.c`'s own comment: "safe from contention because the compositor — the ring's usual consumer — is parked in kwait() while a foreground process runs." True on one core; false the instant the compositor and a foreground reader land on different cores under `smpbalance`. There wasn't even a `preempt_disable()` here — the rings were bare `volatile` — and it would have been useless anyway (it stops a context switch on the local core and means nothing to another). **One `kbd_lock` (`spin_lock_irqsave`) now guards both rings and the state machine.** It is a leaf, held with surgical scope: only across the ring/state touch, never across a blocking wait (`getchar` still `hlt`s with the lock released), never across serial I/O (which touches no keyboard state), and never across `signal_send_foreground` — Ctrl-C/Ctrl-Z now resolve to a signal delivered *after* the unlock, so `kbd_lock` never nests under the process-table locks that path takes. `getkey_poll` releases before calling `getchar_poll`, since the plain test-and-set lock is not reentrant; `scancode_to_ascii` is documented "caller must hold kbd_lock". **Putting a lock in the IRQ path is the delicate part, and irqsave is what makes it safe:** a consumer holding `kbd_lock` has interrupts masked on its own core, so the keyboard IRQ cannot re-enter and self-deadlock there; on another core the IRQ handler just spins the few instructions until the consumer releases. **Verified on a 4-CPU boot, and the harness leads with the strongest integrity gate available: it logs in by typing.** If the locked producer->ring->consumer path dropped or garbled a byte, `nyx`/`nyx` would not authenticate and `[LOGIN] OK` would never print — it did. Then, under `smpbalance on` interleaved with `smpuser` process spreading, it typed `cat /home/nyx/welcome.txt` (a long, exact path where one dropped character prints "No such file") six times: **6 issued, 6 successful "Welcome to NyxOS" reads, 0 command/file errors, 0 faults / 0 panics**, shell fully responsive afterward. No deadlock from the IRQ-path lock, no corruption under cross-core contention. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — internal locking, plus one now-accurate `syscall.c` comment). Next T1 target: net (`net.c` / `ip.c`, still `preempt_disable()`-guarded). |
| v5.9.22 | **VFS mount-table spinlock — second increment of the T1 SMP-locking arc.** With the inode pool locked (v5.9.21), the next shared VFS structure is the **mount table** (`mount_table[8]` + `mount_count`) — read by `vfs_find_mount` on essentially every path operation (it is the first check in `vfs_open`: "does this path fall under a mount?") and written by `vfs_mount`. Both were unlocked. Two concurrent `vfs_mount` calls would each compute the same `&mount_table[mount_count]`, fill the same slot, and both increment — losing one mount and double-counting the other; and a reader iterating `[0, mount_count)` could stride onto a half-written entry the instant the count is bumped, since `mount_count` is a plain `int` with no barrier between "fill the slot" and "publish the count". **The fix leans on an invariant as much as on the lock: the table is append-only.** Nothing removes or rewrites an entry — there is no `vfs_unmount`, `mount_count` only ever increments — so once an entry is counted it is immutable for the life of the system. That is exactly what makes `vfs_find_mount` returning `&mount_table[i]`, which every caller then dereferences *after the function has returned and released any lock*, safe: the pointee never changes under them. So the new `mount_table_lock` (`spin_lock_irqsave`) only has to serialize the append against a second appender and against readers crossing the count-bump — a tiny critical section (at most 8 short `strncmp`s on the read side, `MAX_MOUNT_POINTS` being 8). It is a **leaf lock, and deliberately kept one:** `vfs_mount` releases it *before* calling `ensure_mount_stub` (which allocates a ramdisk node and so takes `node_pool_lock`), so the two VFS locks introduced by this arc are never nested and no lock-ordering hazard exists. **Verified on a 4-CPU boot with `smpbalance on`.** The boot itself exercises the locked writer (`/mnt` is mounted through `vfs_mount`) and the no-match reader (every ramdisk file open during bring-up runs the lookup and falls through). Then, driven over the QEMU monitor: `layout us` (the guest boots the ES layout, where the physical `/` key otherwise yields `-`), then four rounds of `ls /mnt` + `mkdir /mnt/dN` — the **match branch**, routing through the locked lookup into the mounted ext2 — interleaved with `smpuser` process spreading across the APs. The mount keeps routing (the created `d1..d4` dirs list back correctly; 12 `/mnt` listings observed), the APs stay live (`[spin] ring-3 alive`), **0 faults / 0 panics**. As with v5.9.21, correctness is ultimately by construction — the append-only invariant plus a short leaf section — because the concurrent-append race cannot be forced deterministically on demand. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — internal locking). Next T1 targets: the terminal line discipline, then net. |
| v5.9.21 | **VFS node-pool spinlock — first increment of the T1 SMP-locking arc.** Getting here was as much investigation as code. The recommended T3 "trio" turned out **already done**: the fault handler (`isr.c`, the most dangerous `get_current_process()` user) guards every deref with `cur ?`, the syscall-path callers (`do_clone`, `mmap`) all check NULL, and `tlb_shootdown`'s `sti` is deliberate and correct (enabling IF only for the shootdown, restoring the caller's exact IF — the alternative is deadlock). A quiet-host repro of the T2 fork/exec wedge — `smpbalance on` + `smpuser`×5 (40 spawns) + `smpstress` (1.15M allocator ops) — did **not** wedge (0 faults), but that proves nothing for a load-dependent race (the P0.1 trap, [[load-dependent-races]]), so the `smpbalance` warning was left standing rather than softened on weak evidence. With T3 done and T2 needing a load-hunt, the user steered to **T1: real locks so `smpbalance` is genuinely safe.** The allocators (`page_lock`, `kmalloc_lock`) and scheduler (`sched_lock`) were already spinlocked; VFS/terminal/net were not. This locks the first VFS structure: the **inode/node pool** (`nodes[]` + the `free_nodes[]` free-list). `alloc_node`/`free_node` guarded it with `preempt_disable()`, which stops a context switch on the local core but means *nothing* to another core — so two cores could read the same `free_node_count` and hand one node to two files (the exact race `free_node`'s own comment warns of). Replaced with a `spin_lock_irqsave(&node_pool_lock)` leaf lock (no nested lock or allocator inside it); `irqsave`'s `cli` also subsumes the single-core reentrancy `preempt_disable` provided, since it blocks the preempting timer tick. **Verified** on a 4-CPU boot with `smpbalance on` and four rounds of file churn (ls/mkdir/touch/cat/rm) interleaved with `smpuser` process spreading: VFS keeps working, APs stay live, 0 faults / 0 panics. Correctness is by construction (short leaf critical section) since the concurrent-`alloc_node` race can't be forced deterministically. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — internal locking). Next T1 targets: the VFS mount table, then terminal and net. |
| v5.9.20 | **Per-CPU IST stacks — first increment of the kernel-internals track (pivot away from the visual arc).** The double-fault (IST1) and NMI (IST2) exception stacks were a *single* region each, broadcast into all 8 cores' TSS by the old `tss_set_ist()` — a flaw the code itself documented as an "accepted limit": two cores taking a double fault or NMI at the same instant would land on the same stack and corrupt each other's recovery. On a 4-core boot that is a real SMP correctness hole, not a hypothetical. Fixed properly: `tss_set_ist()` (broadcast) is replaced by `tss_set_ist_cpu(cpu, idx, top)` (one core), and kmain now allocates **`cpu_count * 2` IST stacks** (DF + NMI per online core, 8 KB each) and points each core's TSS at its own — done after `smp_init()`, so `cpu_count` is known, and before any AP can fault (unchanged ordering). Stacks grow down, so each TSS slot gets the high end of its 8 KB region. **Verified on a 4-CPU QEMU boot** (`-smp 4`): the SMP bringup finds 3 APs (`4 CPU(s) online`), and the new boot line reports `IST stacks: 4 CPU(s) x 2 (DF+NMI); CPU0 DF=0x…20cb60 CPU3 DF=0x…218b60` — the two DF stacks are 0xC000 apart (exactly 6 × 8 KB, the expected stride), proving they're distinct per core. Full boot to desktop, screendump OK, **0 faults / 0 panics / 0 double-faults**. This closes the "shared IST" item of the T3 hardening trio; the AP `get_current_process()`-NULL and `tlb_shootdown` IF-in-#PF items, plus the T2 fork/exec-under-`smpbalance` wedge and T1 SMP locking, remain. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — boot-path hardening). |
| v5.9.19 | **README hero screenshot refreshed — the repo's public face finally matches the OS.** `gui.png` was dated **2026-07-21**, from before the entire v5.9.10-18 visual arc: it showed square windows, a *blue* Terminal title bar (the pre-brand accent), flat title strips, no drop shadows, and a flat taskbar — a genuinely different, worse-looking desktop than what NyxOS is now. Regenerated from a fresh headless boot of the v5.9.19 build (0 faults): the new shot shows the full current chrome — rounded windows on all four corners, soft drop shadows, top-lit gradient title bars, the purple brand Menu button and gradient taskbar, the Nightfall moon and wallpaper. `gui.png` replaced (11.7 KB -> 21.2 KB, still trivially small), and the README's cache-buster bumped `?v=3` -> `?v=4` so the new image actually shows on GitHub rather than serving the stale cached one. This is the capstone of the visual track (v5.9.1-19): branding, palette, window chrome, tiling, keyboard management, and depth are all shipped and now accurately advertised. **No code, no new syscalls** (still 54 — `gui.png` + README only). |
| v5.9.18 | **Windows round on all four corners — the v5.9.12 follow-up, closed with a framebuffer clip region.** Since v5.9.12 only the window *tops* rounded, because app draw callbacks (`terminal_win_draw`, etc.) fill their client rect edge-to-edge and would square off any rounded bottom. The clean fix is the one v5.9.17 set up: a **clip region** in `fb.c`. `fb_set_round_clip(x,y,w,h,r)` / `fb_clear_clip()` install a rectangle whose two BOTTOM corners are rounded to `r` (the top stays square — the title bar rounds that); `fb_fill_rect` and `fb_blit` mask their writes to it via a per-row `clip_span()` that narrows by `fb_corner_inset` within `r` of the bottom. The compositor wraps each window's **body fill + `win->draw()`** in the clip (and reorders the app draw *before* the frame), so whatever an app paints is masked to the window's rounded-bottom silhouette; `draw_window_frame` then traces the bottom arc unclipped, mirroring the top. **The risk was touching `fb_fill_rect` — the hottest primitive — so the clip-off path is kept byte-for-byte identical and that was proven, not asserted:** the login screen (which never sets a clip) screendumps **identical to the pre-clip build** (0 differing bytes), so nothing on the fast path moved. The desktop then shows every window as a full rounded card — an 8x crop of the Terminal's bottom-right shows the black content curving to the arc, the accent border tracing it, wallpaper through the corner — with terminal text intact (no interior clipping), shadows/gradients untouched, 0 faults. Font glyphs write the framebuffer directly and are deliberately left unclipped (text never reaches the corner arcs). **Landed in one increment, not the two estimated.** **No new syscalls** (still 54 — `fb.c` capability + compositor wiring). |
| v5.9.17 | **Rounded-corner primitives consolidated into `fb.c` — paying down the debt v5.9.16 flagged.** By v5.9.16 the rounding logic existed in **three** copies: the compositor's `corner_inset` + `fill_round_rect` + `stroke_round_rect`, its `bg_isqrt` (for the moon), and login.c's `li_*` clones (needed because a standalone pre-compositor screen can't see the compositor's `static` code). All of it is pure maths over `fb_fill_rect`/`fb_put_pixel`, so it belongs in `fb.c` next to the other framebuffer primitives. This lifts `fb_isqrt`, `fb_corner_inset`, `fb_fill_round_rect`, and `fb_stroke_round_rect` there as public functions (declared in `kernel.h`); the compositor and login.c now call those, and `bg_fill_circle` (the Nightfall moon) shares the one `fb_isqrt` too. **Net: three copies collapse to one, ~70 lines of duplication gone, the binary a touch smaller.** This is a pure refactor — no pixel should move — and that was **proven, not assumed**: the login screen, which exercises all four lifted functions (`fill_round_rect` for the card, `corner_inset` for the header, `stroke_round_rect` for the border, `isqrt` underneath), screendumps **byte-for-byte identical** to v5.9.16 (2359312 bytes, 0 differing). The desktop re-verified visually unchanged (rounded window tops, gradients, shadows all intact), 0 faults. A `sed`-driven rename kept the call sites mechanical; the removed definitions were checked to leave no dangling references (`bg_isqrt`/`corner_inset`/`li_*` all gone). **No new syscalls** (still 54 — internal refactor, no behaviour change). |
| v5.9.16 | **Login card — the brand look reaches the first screen the user sees.** Six increments polished the desktop chrome (v5.9.10-15) but the login panel — the *first* surface on boot — was still a flat rectangle with a plain title. It is now a rounded card with an accent-gradient brand header, matching the desktop it leads into. **The interesting constraint is that `login.c` is a standalone pre-compositor screen:** it runs before `compositor_run()` enables double buffering (so it draws straight to the hardware LFB) and it cannot see the compositor's `static` corner/rounding code. Rather than couple the two files, it grows small local helpers — `li_isqrt` + `li_inset` + `li_fill_round` + `li_stroke_round` — pure maths over `fb_fill_rect`/`fb_put_pixel`. The gradient header reuses the already-public `fb_fill_vgrad`-era primitives (`font_draw_string_trans` for the transparent title), so only the corner code is duplicated; a shared gfx primitive to retire both this copy and the compositor's is noted in-code as a future cleanup. **The header is sized to the old title+divider band (34 px), so nothing below it moved** — the username/password fields, avatar picker (create mode), and hints keep their exact offsets, which matters because `draw_active_field` repaints individual fields by hard-coded geometry on every keystroke. The panel is a full **four-corner** rounded card (it floats centred, like the context menus), title flipped to white on the purple gradient, border a rounded brand-purple stroke. **Verified headlessly with no temp force needed** — the login screen shows before credentials are entered, so the harness just boots, waits for `[LOGIN] Ready`, and screendumps: the card shows the gradient "NyxOS Login" header, rounded corners with the background gradient through the curve, and the brand-purple active username field; 0 faults. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — `login.c` only). |
| v5.9.15 | **Context + user menus polished — every menu now shares one look.** The right-click context menu and the taskbar user-badge popup were the last two flat, hover-less panels; they now match the start menu (v5.9.14) and the rest of the chrome. Because both float freely (unlike the start menu, which anchors to the taskbar), this adds **all-four-corner** rounding: two new reusable helpers, `fill_round_rect(x,y,w,h,R,col)` (rounded body, corner-outside pixels left unpainted) and `stroke_round_rect(...)` (matching 1px outline, four arcs), factor the rounding out of the per-menu code. Both menus gain a **hovered-entry highlight** (accent gradient + `THEME_ON_ACCENT` label) driven by the existing `ctx_menu_item_hit` / `user_menu_item_hit`, which were forward-declared since they sit below their draw functions. The **user popup's header** went from an off-brand `fb_rgb(60,60,90)` blue-grey to the accent gradient (rounded to the body's top corners), so the avatar + username sit on the same brand purple as every other header. The v5.9.14 redraw-on-move was widened from `start_menu_open` to **all three menus** (`start || ctx || user`), so each one's highlight tracks the cursor rather than freezing. **Verified with a temporary boot-time force** (both menus opened, `mouse_set_pos` over a context entry — menus only open on clicks the headless harness can't do): the screendump shows the context menu with four rounded corners and "New File" lit in accent under the cursor, and the user popup with its rounded body + accent-gradient "nyx" header; temp force removed and the clean build re-verified (0 faults). **No new syscalls** (still 54 — compositor only). |
| v5.9.14 | **Start menu depth + hover — the last flat panel joins the polished chrome.** The Start menu was the one surface still reading as a flat rectangle with no hover feedback; it now matches the windows and taskbar and, more importantly, responds to the pointer. **Three changes.** (1) The brand header goes from a flat accent block to the accent **gradient** (`vgrad_row`, matching the taskbar Menu button and title bars) with a transparent label so the gradient shows through the text. (2) The whole menu gets **rounded top corners** via the same `corner_inset` fills as the windows — body and header rounded together, border traced around the arc — while the bottom stays square where it sits flush on the taskbar, so the menu reads as growing out of the bar. (3) A **hovered-entry highlight**: the item under the pointer is filled with the accent gradient and its label flips to `THEME_ON_ACCENT`, computed from the existing `start_menu_item_hit(mouse_x, mouse_y)`. **The hover needed an event-loop change to actually track the cursor:** a bare pointer move only re-blits the existing frame + cursor (`moved` triggers `fb_present` but not `redraw_all`), so the highlight would have frozen; a one-line `if (moved && start_menu_open) redraw = 1;` recomposites on move while the menu is open. **Verified by a temporary boot-time force** (`start_menu_open = 1` + `mouse_set_pos` over an entry, since the menu only opens on a mouse click the headless harness can't reliably do): the screendump shows the gradient "NyxOS Menu" header, rounded top corners with wallpaper through the curve, and the hovered "About" entry lit in accent purple under the cursor; the temp force was then removed and the clean build re-verified (0 faults, menu closed by default). A stale `/* green header band */` comment (the header has been purple for many versions) was corrected in passing. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — compositor only). |
| v5.9.13 | **Taskbar depth — the same gradient treatment carried down from the window chrome to the last flat surface.** After the window trilogy (shadow/gradient/rounded), the taskbar was the one slab still reading as flat; now it sits with everything else. The bar itself is a subtle top-lit vertical gradient (`fb_fill_vgrad` on `taskbar_bg`, ±~11%) with a 1px highlight line along its top edge instead of the old grey rule. A new `draw_grad_button()` helper — gradient fill centred on a base colour, plus a **transparent-background** label (`font_draw_string_trans`) so the text sits on the gradient with no flat panel behind it — factors the title-bar look into a reusable button and is used for the tabs and the Menu button. **The Menu button becomes the brand launcher:** it now always wears the accent purple (dimmed via `col_darken(THEME_ACCENT, 22)` when the menu is closed, full-strength when open) rather than blending into the bar. **The window tabs echo their windows:** a focused tab wears the accent gradient (matching that window's title bar, so the eye ties tab -> window), an unfocused one a quiet bar gradient, a minimized one a flatter grey so it reads as dormant — all with transparent labels. The user-badge name went transparent too, so it blends with the gradient rather than carrying a flat swatch. `draw_button` (a flat-fill helper) had exactly one caller, the old Menu button, so it was removed rather than left as dead code. **Verified on the live desktop:** a 2x crop of the taskbar shows the purple Menu button, the quiet Welcome/About tabs, and the accent-purple focused Terminal tab, all with clean text and a cohesive gradient bar; the full desktop reads as one unified purple theme (Menu + focused tab + focused window all accent); 0 faults. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — compositor only). |
| v5.9.12 | **Rounded window tops — the capstone of the modern-window look (shadow + gradient + rounded corners).** Every window's two top corners are now rounded, so the chrome reads as a soft card rather than a hard rectangle. **The architecture forced the approach.** There is no saved pre-window framebuffer to carve corners against, so the fills simply **never paint the corner-outside pixels** — whatever the back-to-front compositor already drew there (wallpaper, or a lower window) shows through. `corner_inset(d, R)` returns the per-row horizontal inset of a radius-`R` arc (via the existing `bg_isqrt`), and `draw_titlebar` now fills the gradient **row by row**, insetting the first `R` rows. `win_radius()` shrinks the radius for tiny windows so a 120-wide minimum window can't round so hard the corners meet. **Only the top is rounded, and that is a deliberate scope line, not laziness:** app draw callbacks (`terminal_win_draw`, etc.) fill their client rect edge-to-edge, so rounding the bottom would need every app clipped to the corner — a much larger change. Rounding the title bar, which the compositor owns end to end, is clean and self-contained; square bottoms are a recognised window style anyway. The frame follows suit: straight top between the corners, sides starting below the arc, square bottom, and two `corner_inset`-traced arcs hugging one pixel outside the fill so the accent border stays continuous around the curve. **A real artifact was found and fixed by looking, not assuming:** a 6x crop of the top-right corner showed the v5.9.10 drop shadow bleeding through the fresh corner cutout as a stray dark step (the rectangular shadow, drawn first, was no longer fully covered once the corner was cut away). The fix starts the shadow at the title/body junction instead of the window top, so it never reaches into the rounded region while the visible drop (the body's right edge and bottom) is untouched — re-verified clean at the same zoom. **Verified on the live desktop:** all three boot windows show clean quarter-circle top corners with the wallpaper through the cutout, the accent border tracing the arc, the min/max/close buttons intact under the top-right curve, gradients and shadows cohesive, 0 faults. **Follow-up:** rounding the bottom corners needs app-content clipping (noted, deferred). **No new syscalls** (still 54 — compositor only). |
| v5.9.11 | **Title-bar gradient — the second depth cue, sitting with v5.9.10's shadows.** Every window's title bar, flat since forever, now carries a subtle top-lit vertical gradient (lighter at the top edge, darker at the bottom) so the chrome has a little dimension instead of reading as a solid slab. Two new primitives make it clean rather than hacky. `fb_fill_vgrad(x, y, w, h, top, bottom)` in `fb.c` fills a rect by interpolating the colour per row — the framebuffer's first gradient fill, alongside the v5.9.10 blend op. And `font_draw_string_trans()` in `font.c` draws text with a **transparent** background (only glyph pixels written), which is the load-bearing piece: `font_draw_char` fills every non-glyph pixel with a flat `bg`, so drawing the title over the gradient the old way would stamp a solid rectangle behind the text and flatten the very gradient it sits on. With transparent text the gradient shows between and around the letters. **The gradient is centred on the base colour** — `col_lighten(base, amt)` at the top, `col_darken(base, amt)` at the bottom, both proportional so the hue is preserved — which means the mid rows (where the text sits) are ~base and the transparent text needs no matching background to blend. Focused bars get `amt=16`, inactive `amt=8`, so the active window still reads as brighter/more-saturated purple while the greyed inactive bars stay quiet. The title-bar buttons keep their own solid red/green/grey squares (distinct by design) on top of the gradient. **Verified on the live desktop:** an 8x crop of the focused Terminal bar shows a smooth top-to-bottom sheen with the "Terminal" text clean over it and no flat panel behind it; inactive bars show the same effect muted; the v5.9.10 shadows are undisturbed; 0 faults. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — `fb.c` + `font.c` + compositor only). |
| v5.9.10 | **Window drop shadows — the first depth in the compositor, a fresh visual area after four increments of window management.** Every window now casts a soft shadow onto the desktop and the windows beneath it, so the stack reads as layered instead of flat. The enabling primitive is `fb_darken_rect(x, y, w, h, shade)` in `fb.c`: it *reads* the current draw target and mixes each pixel toward black by `shade/255`, so it composites over whatever was already drawn — the first blend op in a framebuffer that until now only had opaque fills and blits. It is 32bpp-only and a silent no-op otherwise (shadows are cosmetic, never correctness), with signed clipped coordinates so a shadow feathered past the top-left origin is fine. **The compositing order made this almost free to place correctly:** `redraw_all` already draws windows back-to-front, so casting each window's shadow *right before the window itself* lands it on the desktop and all lower windows, and the window then paints opaque on top — leaving only the soft band past the bottom/right edges visible, which is the whole drop-shadow look. No z-order bookkeeping, no separate shadow pass. **The shape was tuned by measurement, not guesswork.** A first pass of three offset filled rects gave a real but *narrow* shadow — sampling the back buffer showed a solid 26% darken but only a ~5px visible band (`(74,51,119)` vs `(100,69,161)` wallpaper), reading as a hard line rather than a soft shadow. The rewrite draws concentric 1px **outlines** growing outward from the offset window rect (OFFSET 6, RADIUS 12, CORE 100 fading linearly to 0): each pixel is darkened exactly once so the gradient is clean with no compounding, and the cost is *perimeter, not area* — a handful of thin strips per window instead of twelve full-window fills. Maximised windows skip it (they fill the usable area, so the shadow would be entirely behind them or off-screen); snapped and normal windows all cast. **Verified on the live desktop:** the three boot windows show soft ~12px feathered shadows falling correctly onto each other and the wallpaper, gradient confirmed smooth in an 8x crop, 0 faults. **No new syscalls** (still 54 — `fb.c` + compositor only). |
| v5.9.9 | **Quarter-tiling — v5.9.8's snap grown from one axis to two.** The half-snaps become a 2-axis grid: a horizontal side (Alt+Left/Right) and a vertical zone (Alt+Up/Down), so a window can sit in either half *or* any of the four corners. Four new states `WSTATE_SNAP_TL/TR/BL/BR` join the two halves, but the state field never grew unwieldy because four tiny accessors carry all the meaning — `win_is_snapped`, `win_snap_left`, `win_snap_vzone` (0 full / 1 top / 2 bottom), and `win_snap_state(left, vzone)` to compose one back.
